Acta MyologicaActa MyolSearch for new CSF biomarkers in SMA patients treated with Nusinersen
PanicucciC BartolucciM SahinE RussoR VallettaM BrolattiN PedemonteM CasaliniS BarattoS D’AmicoA PaneM SframeliM StancanelliC MessinaS AlbamonteE SansoneV MercuriE ChamberyA SezermanU PetrettoA BrunoC 1 Center of Translational and Experimental Myology, IRCCS Istituto Giannina Gaslini, Genoa, Italy2 Core Facilities-Clinical Proteomics and Metabolomics, IRCCS Istituto Giannina Gaslini, Genoa, Italy3 Department of Biostatistics and Medical Informatics, School of Medicine, Acibadem Mehmet Ali Aydinlar University, Istanbul, Turkey4 Department of Environmental, Biological and Pharmaceutical Sciences and Technologies, University of Campania “Luigi Vanvitelli”, Caserta, Italy5 Unit of Neuromuscular and Neurodegenerative Disorders, Bambino Gesù Children’s Hospital IRCCS, Rome, Italy6 Centro Clinico Nemo, Fondazione Policlinico Universitario Agostino Gemelli IRCCS, Rome, Italy7 Department of Clinical and Experimental Medicine, University of Messina, Messina, Italy8 Neurorehabilitation Unit, Centro Clinico NeMO, University of Milan, Milan, Italy9

## Abstract
The availability of disease modifying therapies and newborn screening programs for spinal muscular atrophy (SMA) have generated an urgent need to identify reliable biomarkers that provide insight into disease progression, therapeutic response and classify patients according to disease severity.
We applied an unsupervised proteomic approach, by liquid chromatography mass spectroscopy (LC-MS) to analyze the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) protein profile of 61 SMA patients (SMA1 n = 19, SMA2 n = 19, SMA3 n = 23) treated with nusinersen, in order i) to define nusinersen-induced proteomic changes after 1 year of treatment, and ii) to identify meaningful differences in proteomic expression at baseline. Bioinformatics analysis was performed to investigate Gene Ontology (GO) functional annotation and Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes (KEGG) enriched signaling pathways of differentially expressed proteins (DEPs) after treatment. A machine learning classifier approach (Random Forest, RF) was also applied to exploit proteins able to stratify disease severity at baseline.
Nusinersen-induced changes on proteomic profile were consistent with i) common effects in all SMA types (i.e. regulation of axonogenesis and complement system), and ii) disease severity-specific changes, namely regulation of glucose metabolism in SMA1, regulation of coagulation processes in SMA2, and regulation of peptidase activity in SMA3.
The RF algorithm, based on protein expression at baseline, identified different proteins that discriminate SMA1 patients from the less severe forms.
These findings provide new insights on CSF nusinersen biological effects in a large cohort of SMA patients, and identify new potential disease severity biomarkers.

Collection date 2023 Jun.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolPregnancy experience in women with spinal muscular atrophy: an overview in the cohort of the University of Naples “Federico II’’
BencivengaRP ZoppiD RussoA CassanoE TozzaS IodiceR DubbiosoR ManganelliF RuggieroL 1Department of Neurosciences, Reproductive and Odontostomatological Sciences, University of Naples “Federico II”, Naples, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Female SMA patients reach fertile age with a different clinical picture. Many patients with SMA Type III are ambulatory during pregnancy, while the totality of women with Type II phenotype are wheelchair-bound. Little data is available about the possible effects of pregnancy on SMA course. In this study, we analyzed outcomes in the different stages of the pregnancy, with a special focus on maternal and fetal complications, mode of delivery, anesthesiological risk and respiratory function.
Our cohort is compound by 10 SMA female (7 SMA III and 2 SMA II). The number of pregnancies was 9 (six term-pregnancy, three abortion). No one had medical assisted procreation. All have done genetic counseling, one of which was pre-conception. Two SMA II patients had two pregnancies each, all of which resulted in pre-term labor. Two SMA III patients had one pregnancy each, resulted in full-term labor. All pregnancies resulted in cesarian delivery performed under epidural anesthesia, except one under general anesthesia. Two patients referred motor symptoms worsening after delivery and chronic back pain during pregnancy. One had pneumonia after delivery. In three cases there were birth complications, but newborn outcomes were all healthy. Only two patients breastfedOur experience testify that a successful pregnancy is possible with SMA. Anyway, particularly the sitter patients experienced pregnancy or birth complications. A correct approach should include a standardized multidisciplinary team, with an obstetrician, neurologist, neonatologist, genetist, anesthesiologist, pneumologist and trained nurses, Our results focus on the need to consider motherhood experience in the management of SMA female patients and in the next future we will include the currently disease modifying therapies in this taking charge.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolPatients’ and caregivers’ expectations on possible functional changes following disease-modifying treatment in Spinal Muscular Atrophy
PeraMC CorattiG CasiraghiJL BravettiC FedeliA StrikaM AlbamonteE AntonaciL RossiD PaneM SansoneV MercuriE 1 Department of life science and public health, Pediatric Neurology, Università Cattolica del Sacro Cuore, Rome, Italy2 The NEMO Center in Rome, Fondazione Policlinico Universitario Agostino Gemelli IRCCS, Rome, Italy3 The NEMO Center in Milan, Neurorehabilitation Unit, University of Milan, ASST Niguarda Hospital, Milan, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
The aim of this study was to explore current patients’ and caregivers’ expectations on possible functional changes following treatment in comparison to the pre-pharmacological eraThe same questionnaire, previously used in 2016, was administered to caregivers of SMA individuals, irrespective of the age of the patients, and to patients over the age of 13. The questionnaire focus on 1) caregivers and patients expectations 2) meaningfulness of functional motor scales 3) their willingness to be enrolled in a clinical trial. A comparative study was performed with data obtained using the same questionnaire soon before the advent of disease-modifying therapiesThe new cohort includes 45 patients and 150 caregivers, these also including the caregivers of the 45 patients. No difference was found in responses between patients and their caregivers. When comparing current data to those obtained in 2016, the most obvious differences were related to disease perception over the last year (stability: 16.5% in 2016 vs 43.6% in 2022; deterioration 70.5% vs 12.8%, improvement: 12.9% vs 43.6%) and expectations from clinical trials with higher expectations in motor achievements in 2022 compared to 2016 (p < 0.001)This study has allowed us to record significant changes in patients’ and caregivers’ expectations in relation to the new pharmacological therapies. These results expand our knowledge on the impact of the disease on patients and caregivers and may be used at time of trial design and in the interpretation of the real world data.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolSwallowing and feeding in Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA) type 1: the SFERA project and the path towards a diagnostic and treatment algorithm
GandolfiS DosiC ScopellitiMR MinacapilliE De AmicisR BertoliS MandelliA CampariA SchindlerA MassonRon behalf of the SFERA working group 1 Developmental Neurology Unit, Department of Pediatric Neurosciences, Fondazione IRCCS Istituto Neurologico Carlo Besta, Milan, Italy2 International Center for the Assessment of Nutritional Status (ICANS), Department of Food, Environmental and Nutritional Sciences (DeFENS), Università di Milano, Milan, Italy3 Laboratory of Nutrition and Obesity Research, Department of Endocrine and Metabolic Diseases, IRCCS Istituto Auxologico Italiano, Milan, Italy4 Terapia Intensiva Pediatrica e Cure Palliative Pediatriche, V. ## Abstract
Spinal muscular atrophy (SMA) is a progressive disease characterized by spinal and bulbar motor neurons degeneration, caused by homozygous deletion of SMN1 gene. Type 1 is the severest form of SMA, with symptoms onset before 6 months of age. 8% of untreated patients survive 20 months of life and most of them required a complete feeding support within 12 months. The phenotypes of treated children range from severe to mild clinical conditions, related to the age at treatment and individual response. The effect on swallowing and nutritional status is still poorly investigated, because of the lack of easy and sensitive methods to assess them and the incomplete understanding of biological mechanisms of swallowing impairment, involving deficits in sucking and soft palate elevation, tongue base retraction, reduced lingual strength, impaired pharyngeal and hypolaryngeal movements, incomplete upper esophageal segment openingWe applied a clinical protocol, including standardized evaluations of swallowing (OrSAT, P-FOIS, FILS, MAS) and nutritional status, to a population of more than 30 treated SMA 1 patients. A subgroup of patients underwent instrumental assessment, including VFSS (videofluoroscopic swallowing study) and FEES (fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation of swallowing). Few patients have a follow-up at 12 months. All the evaluations were discussed within the SFERA working group, to define a complete profile of the patient before making any therapeutic or rehabilitative decision. We found concordance across the assessments, with differences in detecting subtler clinical featuresWe discuss the application of easy-to-perform tests for swallowing and feeding, with the goal of speeding up diagnosis, reduce invasive examinations, detecting potential measures of drugs efficacy and rehabilitation.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolFacial nerve vulnerability in spinal muscular atrophy and motor unit number index (MUNIX) of the orbicularis oculi muscle
BarpA CarraroE SalminF LizioA CheliM SansoneV 1 NeMO Clinical Center, University of Milan, Niguarda Hospital, Milano, Italy2 NeMO Clinical Center in Trento, Villa Rosa Rehabilitation Hospital, Azienda Sanitaria per i Servizi Sanitari, Pergine Valsugana (Trento), Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Measures for assessing cranial nerve vulnerability in spinal muscular atrophy (SMA) have not been determined yet. Motor unit number index (MUNIX) studies have shown correlations with disease severity but have been used only in limb muscles. In the present study we explore facial nerve response, MUNIX and motor unit size index (MUSIX) of the orbicularis oculi muscle in a cohort of patients with SMAFacial nerve response (measured as compound muscle action potential, CMAP), MUNIX and MUSIX of the orbicularis oculi muscle were cross-sectionally recorded in patients with SMA and compared to healthy control subjects (HCs). Active maximum mouth opening (aMMO) was also measured at baseline in our SMA cohort37 patients with SMA (21 SMA II; 16 SMA III) and 27 HCs were recruited. CMAP of the facial nerve and MUNIX of orbicularis oculi proved to be feasible and well tolerated techniques. CMAP amplitude and MUNIX scores were significantly lower in patients with SMA compared to HCs (p < .0001). Both MUNIX and CMAP amplitude were significantly higher in patients with SMA III compared to SMA II. No significant difference emerged comparing CMAP amplitude, MUNIX and MUSIX scores between those with different functional status or nusinersen treatmentOur results provide neurophysiological evidence of facial nerve and muscle involvement in patients with SMA. CMAP of the facial nerve and MUNIX of orbicularis oculi show a high accuracy in discriminating between the various subtypes of SMA and in quantifying the motor unit loss of the facial nerve.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolMolecular and biochemical characterization of patient-derived cellular models for Spinal Muscular Atrophy with Myoclonus Epilepsy (SMA-PME)

## Abstract
Spinal Muscular Atrophy with Myoclonus Epilepsy (SMA-PME) is a rare disorder featuring muscle weakness, finally leading to fatal respiratory paralysis and intractable myoclonic epilepsy. Affected patients harbor mutations in ASAH1 encoding for the acid ceramidase, the enzyme deputed to the catabolism of ceramide and complex sphingolipids inside lysosomes. ASAH1 mutations also cause a severe pediatric multisystemic phenotype, Farber lipogranulomatosis. Pathogenetic mechanisms underlining these disorders are still unclearWe have performed an extensive molecular and biochemical characterization of fibroblasts obtained from 5 SMA-PME and 3 Farber patientsCompared to controls, protein analysis documented altered expression and stability of ASAH1 both as protein precursor and mature heterodimer in non-reducing experiments. Impaired acid ceramidase activity, evaluated by spectrophotometric analysis and in-vivo labelling as well as ceramide accumulation were found in patients’ cells and correlated with disease severity. Gene expression analysis disclosed key differences in sphingolipid metabolism. Markers of apoptosis were unaltered. We also generated and characterized motor neurons from induced pluripotent stem cells established from a SMA-PME patientNo therapy is available for disorders due to acid ceramidase deficiency. Experimental therapies based on enzyme replacement strategies or gene therapy approaches are currently under developmentThese cellular models will be used to investigate disease pathogenesis and to contribute to therapeutic advancement for this rare form of Spinal Muscular Atrophy.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolEngineering human stem cells for advanced neuromuscular disease and therapy modelling
LionelloVM PintonL DastidarS MooreD ChoiSW Steele-StallardH JalalS JiangY ZammitP TedescoFS 1 Department of Cell and Developmental Biology, University College London, London, UK2
The Francis Crick Institute, 1 Midland Road, London, UK3

## Abstract
Lack of robust, human(ized) models is a major barrier towards development and clinical translation of novel neuromuscular therapies. To tackle this challenge, we developed 3D human models of skeletal muscle tissue, enabling high-fidelity modelling of different morphological and functional defects in tissue compartments impaired in muscular dystrophies. 3D muscles were developed from human primary or immortalised myoblasts, as well as from induced pluripotent stem cells (iPSCs), which were first differentiated into myogenic, neural and vascular progenitor cells, and then combined within 3D biomaterials to generate aligned myofibre scaffolds containing vascular networks and motoneurons. Engineered muscles recapitulated morphological and functional features of human skeletal muscle, including establishment of a pool of muscle stem cells. Importantly, they provided a high-fidelity platform to study muscle pathology, such as emergence of dysmorphic nuclei in muscular dystrophies caused by mutant lamins and identified nuclear elongation as a prevalent phenotypic readout. We then exploited this bioengineering platform to test an antisense-oligonucleotide-based therapeutic strategy on newly-generated iPSC myogenic derivatives from a patient with a rare LMNA mutation. To further validate this approach in another muscle disease with abnormal nuclear dynamics, we developed a 3D model of the centronuclear myopathy caused by mutations in the MTM1 gene, where we identified myofibre atrophy and abnormal calcium dynamics as promising morphological and functional readouts for therapeutic development. Finally, I will discuss current extensions of this technology to other muscular dystrophies and to test next generation genetic therapies, laying the foundations for a multi-functional platform for precision medicine in neuromuscular diseases.

Collection date 2023 Jun.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolSolving the NGS negative cases: improved WES protocols to make increasingly accurate genetic diagnoses
TorellaA OnoreME PicilloE BudillonA PilusoG PolitanoL NigroV 1 Department of Precision Medicine, University Hospital and University of Campania 'Luigi Vanvitelli', Naples, Italy2
Telethon Institute of Genetics and Medicine (TIGEM), Pozzuoli, Naples, Italy3 Cardiomyology and Medical Genetics, Department of Precision Medicine, University Hospital and University of Campania 'Luigi Vanvitelli', Naples, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Since 2010, the introduction of NGS has had a dramatic impact on genetic diagnostics allowing the diagnosis of neuromuscular diseases even with elusive clinical and histopathological manifestations. The Naples group created the first targeted NGS protocols based on highly experimental enrichment systems, such as the MotorPlex. Thousands of undiagnosed patients have been studied with this system [PMID: 25214167]. However, a number of cases remained undiagnosed and, during the years, improved NGS approaches have been developed together with discovery of new disease-causing genesIn this study, a cohort of 29 unresolved neuromuscular patients was recruited from the Medical Genetics and Cardiomyology Unit of the University of Campania Luigi Vanvitelli, Naples. All patients were clinically reclassified and retested by the latest version of whole exome sequencing (WES). Fourteen patients were solved with 47% achieving a conclusive diagnosis. Surprisingly in four, the second variant was identified in CAPN3 (2), GAA and GMPPB genes. Pathogenic variants were identified in two of the 14 patients: a new heterozygous truncating variant in the DAG1 gene and a splicing variant in the DMD gene. Eight cases were clinically reevaluated during routine follow-up and received a definitive molecular diagnosis. Our results suggest that the re-analysis of unsolved patients with additional genetic investigation is crucial to detect pathogenic variants missed in previous NGS analyses and to improve the quality of life of patients affected by muscle diseases. Improvements in whole exome sequencing have enabled us to achieve a better diagnostic rate of 43%. Finally, the identification of the second variant in GAA in a patient with Pompe disease enabled to include him in a therapeutic protocol with acid alpha glucosidase replacement therapy.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA comparison of different NGS-based strategies for the routine clinical diagnosis of neuromuscular disorders
BertolinC BoarettoF VoltaS PegoraroE BelloL SorarùG ViscomiC ZevianiM SalviatiL 1
Genetica Clinica, Azienda Ospedaliera Università Padova, Padova, Italy2 Dipartimento di Scienze Biomediche, Università di Padova, Padova, Italy3
Clinica Neurologica Azienda Ospedaliera Università Padova, Padova, Italy4
IRCCS materno infantile Burlo Garofolo, Trieste, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Next Generation Sequencing has revolutionized the diagnosis of neuromuscular disorders, allowing to study hundreds of genes in hundreds of patients simultaneously, in a fraction of the time and of the costs compared to just a decade ago. Our center performs routine diagnosis for a wide range of disorders: myopathies, peripheral neuropathies, ataxias, spastic paraplegias, ALS, and mitochondrial disordersIn this work, we compare different strategies, based on custom panels, clinical exome, and whole exome sequencing (WES)Modern custom panels may contain up to several hundreds of genes and may target specific genomic regions, that are usually not covered by WES (UTRs, introns, promoters…) as well as mtDNA (at high coverage). Moreover, coverage of “difficult” targets may be improved by optimizing probe design. It is possible to detect some triplet expansions such as the one associated to CANVAS disease.
On the other hand, WES offers the opportunity to study all 20000 human genes, albeit at a much higher cost and in fewer patients simultaneously. Moreover some specific regions such as the first two exons of SURF1 and the whole 3’ region of TYMP, two critical genes for mitochondrial disorders are poorly covered by WES, while coverage with panels is 100% > 20X. Overall, data interpretation is more complex and CNV analysis less efficientClinical exome is a compromise, however it has the limitations of both approaches without any of their specific advantages. In the clinical routine, custom panels may provide a relatively simple first line test, reserving WES for more complex situations.

SelvaticiR NeriM GualandiF BigoniS FortunatoF BertiniE PaneM MercuriE FerliniA 1 Unit of Medical Genetics, Department of Medical Sciences, University of Ferrara, Ferrara, Italy2 Unit of Neuromuscular and Neurodegenerative Diseases, Department of Neurosciences, Bambino Gesù Paediatric Hospital, Rome, Italy3 Paediatric Neurology Unit, Catholic University, Rome, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Within the Solve-RD project we performed Whole Exome or Genome Sequencing re-analysis in 3 families of four with a complex neuromuscular phenotypes and still orphan of molecular diagnosis. The optimized re-analysis allowed to solve the following cases:

- in two affected brothers with spastic paraplegia were identified biallelic variations in PNPT1 gene (p.Ala507Ser, already reported and p.Tyr432Ter, never reported) inherited from healthy parents. A re-evaluation of the phenotype allowed to diagnose Combined oxidative phosphorylation deficiency-13 (OMIM 614932), with clinical manifestations highly heterogeneous and intra-familiar variability- in a male patient with Leber congenital amaurosis, ataxia and cerebellar atrophy were found biallelic variations in WDR73 gene (p.Val203del and p.Asp189ValfsTer6, never reported), inherited from healthy parents. The phenotype is compatible with Galloway-Mowat syndrome 1 (OMIM 251300), with some different clinical features.
- in two affected brothers with congenital arthrogryposis a frameshift variation (p.Arg1010SerfsTer3, never reported) and deletion of exons 1-2 in PRG4 gene were identified. Camptodactyly-arthropathy-coxa vara-pericarditis syndrome is compatible with the identified genotype.
Furthermore, a family of four with an 11 years old affected boy was submitted to SR-WGS. The patient was second child of Gipsy first cousins and presented neonatal generalized hypotonia, subsequent psychomotor retardation impaired autonomous deambulation, and intellectual disability. A variation never reported in THOC2 gene (p.Ile1125Thr) inherited from mother, was identified suggesting a X-linked intellectual developmental disorder-12 (XLID12, # 300957)We conclude that WES and WGS re-analysis was very effective to solve families whose genetic causes has remained undeterminedGrant reference: EU project Solve-RD Grant Agreement number:779257.
Several authors are members of the ERN-NMD – Project ID N°870177 and ERN-ITHACA [EU F.P.A. ID:3HP-HP-FPA ERN-01-2016/739516].

## Abstract
Ryanodine receptor 1-related myopathies (RYR1-RM) are a wide spectrum of disease caused by mutations in RYR1 gene. The histological manifestations are multiple and are commonly used to classify the diseaseIn this study we focus on Central Core Disease (CCD) and Multi-minicore Disease (MmD) due to RYR1 mutations and perform RNA-sequencing analyses of skeletal muscle biopsies obtained from patients to compare their transcriptional profiles with healthy controls.
We performed a DEseq2 bioinformatics analysis on the transcriptome of 17 patients (10 CCD and 6 MmD) and 6 healthy controls. In addition to a direct analysis of the difference in expression of single genes, pathway analyzes (Over-representation and Gene-Set Enrichment Analysis) were performed. Results were validated by RT-qPCRWe found almost no overlap in differentially expressed genes (DEGs) in MmD and CCD compared to controls, despite all patients were RYR1-mutated, highlighting the variety of pathomechanisms underlying the disease. ZFP36 and ATN1 are the only RNAs coding proteins differentially expressed by both MmD and CCD. Our analyses identified the apoptosis as a pathway involved in the disease, shedding light on a possible mechanism in core diseasesOur exploratory analysis showed several deregulated genes in both MmD and CCD. Further experiments are necessary to start to dissect the role of each gene in disease pathophysiology.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolMedaka fish Oryzias latipes a promising model to study titinopathies: the HMERF case study
CetrangoloV SavareseM ConteI PolishchukL Vincenzo NigroV LinariM UddB 1
Telethon Institute of Genetics and Medicine-TIGEM, Pozzuoli (NA), Italy2
Folkhälsan Research Center, Helsinki, Finland. Department of Medical and Clinical Genetics, Medicum, University of Helsinki, Helsinki, Finland3

## Abstract
Titin is the largest protein in nature, composed of more than 35,000 amino acids organized in immunoglobulin-like and fibronectin-type 3 domains, interspersed by unique sequence regions. It has a crucial role in the architecture of sarcomeres in heart and skeletal muscles.
Titin variants cause a wide spectrum of skeletal muscle diseases with or without an overt cardiomyopathy. Among these, an increasing number of variants in the A150 domain, a fibronectin-type 3 domain in the A-band, have been recently associated with HMERF (Hereditary myopathy with early respiratory failure). HMERF is a severe disorder, with onset in early adulthood, manifesting with respiratory failure, and proximal and distal muscle weakness.
While typical vertebrate models (mouse and zebrafish) carrying variants in different titin domains have been developed, a vertebrate model to study HMERF pathomechanism is missing. Oryzias latipes (medaka) is a small fish that is emerging as a promising model to investigate titinopathies. Medaka possess just one copy of titin paralog, and the A150 domain sequence is highly conserved between human and medaka. We generated the first two HMERF vertebrate models, each of them harboring a missense variant found in HMERF patients (C31712R and W31729R). The ultrastructure of the muscles’ fibers elucidates the organization and size of the sarcomeres and myofibrilis, while immunostainings with sarcomere markers will highlight the disruption of the muscle fibers in the generated models. The thorough characterization of these two models will elucidate the genotype-phenotype relation in HMERF disease and pave the way for new possible therapeutic approaches.

BarattoS BenziA AstigianoC SturlaL PanicucciC MamchaoiK RaffaghelloL BruzzoneS GazzerroE BrunoC 1 Center of Translational and Experimental Myology, IRCCS Istituto Giannina Gaslini, Genova, Italy2 Department of Experimental Medicine-DIMES, University of Genova, Genova, Italy3 Sorbonne Université, Inserm, Institut de Myologie, Centre de Recherche en Myologie, Paris, France4 Unit of Muscle Research Experimental and Clinical Research Center, a Cooperation Between the Max-Delbrück-Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association and Charité-Universitätsmedizin, Berlin, Germany5 Department of Neuroscience, Rehabilitation, Ophtalmology, Genetics, Maternal and ChildHealth-DINOGMI, University of Genova, Genova, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Sarcoglycanopathies or Limb-Girdle Muscular Dystrophies (LGMDR3-6) caused by genetic loss-of-function of the membrane proteins sarcoglycans (SGs), are characterized by progressive degeneration of skeletal muscle. Muscle necrosis is associated with immune–mediated damage, whose triggering and perpetuating molecular mechanisms are not fully elucidated yet. Extracellular adenosine triphosphate (eATP) seems to represent a crucial factor, with activation of purinergic receptors. Indeed, in vivo blockade of the eATP/P2X7 purinergic pathway ameliorated muscle disease progression. P2X7 inhibition improved the dystrophic process by restraining the activity on immune cells. In this study, we investigated eATP effects in primary cultures of myoblasts isolated from patients with LGMDR3 (α-sarcoglycanopathy) and in immortalized cells isolated from a patient with LGMDR5 (γ-sarcoglycanopathy). Our results demonstrated that, owing to a reduced ecto-ATPase activity and/or an enhanced release of ATP, patient cells are exposed to increased juxtamembrane concentrations of eATP and display a higher susceptivity to eATP signals. The purinoceptor P2Y2, which proved to be overexpressed in patient cells, was identified as a pivotal receptor responsible for the enhanced ATP-induced or UTP-induced Ca2+ increase in affected myoblasts. Moreover, P2Y2 stimulation in LGMDR3 muscle cells induced chemotaxis of immune cells and release of interleukin-8. In conclusion, a higher eATP concentration and sensitivity in primary human muscle cells carrying different α-SG or γ-SG loss-of-function mutations indicate that eATP/P2Y2 is an enhanced signaling axis in cells from patients. Dissecting the basic players of inflammation in muscular dystrophies may also be critical in overcoming the immunologic hurdles associated with emerging gene therapies for these disorders.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolFunctional characterization suggests that ACTN2 frameshift variants cause distal myopathy through protein aggregation
Ranta-ahoJ JonsonPH SarparantaJ UddB SavareseM 1Folkhalsan Research Center & University of Helsinki, Helsinki, Finland162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. The article can be used by giving appropriate credit and mentioning the license, but only for non-commercial purposes and only in the original version. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Distal myopathies are characterized by progressive muscle weakness primarily presenting in the hands and feet. The disease is genetic in nature and variants in many different genes have been shown to cause distal myopathy. Most recently, variants in ACTN2, a gene previously associated with cardiomyopathy, have been shown to cause a distal myopathy phenotype. ACTN2 encodes alpha-actinin-2, an important structural protein that links actin and titin to the sarcomere Z-disk. In ACTN2-related diseases, actininopathies, numerous variants have been reported as disease-causing, and new variants are continuously discovered. However, the significance of many variants remains uncertain as the molecular mechanisms underlying actininopathies are largely unknown.
Here, functional characterization in C2C12 cell models of several ACTN2 variants is conducted to investigate the molecular mechanisms underlying actininopathies. The assessed variants include both frameshift and missense variants, associated with either dominant or recessive distal myopathy. The results revealed that the frameshift variants generating a protein extension produce alpha-actinin-2 aggregates in the cell model. This finding indicates that the observed aggregation is a molecular mechanism contributing to the disease phenotype associated with these variants. Conversely, the results show that missense variants associated with recessive actininopathy do not cause detectable alpha-actinin-2 aggregates in the cell model.
Altogether, the results indicate that alpha-actinin-2 aggregation is present in some actininopathies, however, this disease mechanism is likely only associated with a limited number of variants. Therefore, to shed light on the unknown pathomechanisms of actiniopathies, alternate methods of functional characterization should be explored in future research.

## Abstract
LAMA2-RD, or Merosin-deficient Congenital Muscular Dystrophy (MD), is one of the most frequent recessively-inherited neuromuscular disorders. It is due to mutations in the LAMA2 gene encoding for the alpha-2 subunit of Laminin-211 (or merosin), which is a major component of the basal lamina of skeletal muscle and other tissues. Patients with LAMA2-RD therefore present with a multisystem disorder primary characterized by muscular dystrophy variably associated with peripheral neuropathy and brain abnormalities. Disease-related complications include progressive respiratory impairment, swallowing difficulties, scoliosis, joint contractures and epilepsy. Largely depending on the complete or partial loss of Lm-211 (i.e. CD vs PD), the disease phenotypes range from a severe congenital muscular dystrophy to a milder, limb-girdle MD.
Novel promising therapeutic approaches are getting closer to clinical application. However, limited knowledge regarding the longitudinal trajectory of different outcome measures and potentially unrecognized regional phenotypic differences hinder the design of clinical trials. Here we describe the largest Italian cohort of LAMA2-RD patients, comprising 71 individuals currently followed by 17 tertiary neuromuscular centers (median age 14.8y; range 2-68y). Of these, 45 were still younger than 18 years (3 < 5 years) at last assessment. Genetic analysis was available for 49 patients (27 individuals were compound heterozygous, 20 homozygous, 2 with a single variant). Muscle biopsy was available in 51 patients (28 CD; 23 PD). Of note partial deficiency had a higher prevalence than previously described (45% vs < 20%). CD patients had a median age of 14.4 years (range 2-30.5), PD 15.3y (range 7.6-68.6).

## Abstract
Nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NAD+) is an essential cofactor whose repletion ameliorates muscular dystrophy by improving the mitochondrial and muscle stem cell function. Indeed, NAD+ deficit represents a deleterious feature of the dystrophic muscle resulting in muscle degeneration. Indeed, the NAD+-ase CD38, the major consumer of extracellular NAD+, was demonstrated to exert a key role in the pathophysiology of Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy. Sarcoglycanopathies (SGCs) are recessive inherited limb-girdle muscular dystrophies due to mutations in sarcoglycan genes, where the role of NAD+/CD38 axis in the pathophysiology is unknown. Our data demonstrate that in skeletal muscles of Sgca-/- mice, NAD(P)(H) and ATP content are significantly lower than in WT animals. NAD+ reduction is associated to a decreased expression of Nampt, the enzyme mediating NAD+ salvage pathway for Nicotinamide, and increased NAD+-ase activity and CD38 expression in muscle infiltrating cells of Sgca-/- mice.
RNA sequencing was performed in 17 patients affected by a-SGC, classified as severe or mild on the basis of clinical parameters, and 10 unaffected individuals. Unsupervised hierarchical clustering analysis of 5000 genes with the highest variance demonstrates that the gene expression profile for severe a-SGC is strongly different compared to controls, whereas mild a-SGC shows no consistent differences to controls. The analysis of genes related to NAD+ metabolism reveals significant downregulation of NAD+ salvage pathway enzymes, whereas NAD+ consuming enzymes are overexpressed in severe a-SGC patients compared to controls. In conclusion, this study provides a first picture of alterations in NAD+-related metabolic pathways, suggesting novel promising therapeutic targets for a-SGC patients.

## Abstract
Biallelic titin truncating variants (TTNtv) cause different forms of muscular dystrophy, including severe titinopathies with a prenatal onset and a multisystemic involvement.
We collected unpublished cases and reviewed previously published cases with biallelic TTNtv to estimate the prevalence of prenatal signs.
Out of 115 patients, 64 have at least one TTNtv in a metatranscript-only exons, 20 have at least one TTNtv in exon 359, 10 have at least one variant in exons 360-363, and 17 have one TTNtv in a canonical exon and one splicing variant. Also, we identified 4 cases with biallelic TTNtv in canonical exons, which are normally considered incompatible with lifeIn the whole cohort, 71 cases out of 115 (61.7%) had prenatal symptoms, most frequently fetal akinesia; 59 (51.3%) showed hypotonia after birth and 86 (74.8%) have joint contractures. Notably, 50 (43.5%) have dysmorphic features. Interestingly, some patients with TTNtv in a metatranscript exon and a splicing variant have an improving clinical progression with growthPrenatal titinopathies are the severe end of the TTN-related spectrum, with variable presentation according to the combination of TTNtv. In addition to the myopathic phenotype, patients show multi-systemic and syndromic-like signs, such as facial dysmorphisms, congenital multiplex arthogryposis, and others. In the general population, approximately one in 100 people carries a heterozygous TTNtv, raising issues on preconception and prenatal counselling. Also, reclassification of canonical and non-canonical exons is warranted to facilitate genotype-phenotype correlation.

## Abstract
The work employed Whole Exome Sequencing (WES) to investigate known and unknown genetic contributors that may be involved in FSHD and represent potential disease modifiers, even in presence of a D4Z4 Reduced Allele (DRA). On this subject, 126 patients with clinical signs of FSHD were included in the study and were characterized by D4Z4 sizing, methylation analysis and WES. In-house protocols were employed for D4Z4 sizing and methylation analysis, whereas the Illumina® Next-Seq 550 system was utilized for WES. In presence of variants of interest, the molecular analysis was extended to the family members. The analysis identified 20 relevant variants in 19 patients. In particular, 14 variants were located in known genes (SMCHD1, DNMT3B and LRIF1), whereas 6 variants were found in novel genes implicated in the DUX4-repressive machinery. Most of them were found together with a permissive short (4-7 RU) or intermediate/long DRA (8-20 RU), suggesting that different genes can contribute to disease heterogeneity in presence of a FSHD permissive backgroundOur results support FSHD as a genetically complex disease, in which variations in known (SMCHD1, DNMT3B, LRIF1) and candidate novel genes could influence the phenotype, penetrance and severity of disease among patients as well as within the same family. Moreover, the study further emphasizes the importance of extending the analysis of molecular findings within the proband’s family, with the purpose of providing a broader framework for understanding single cases and allowing finer genotype-phenotype correlations in FSHD-affected families.

## Abstract
The search for reduced-size D4Z4 alleles is indicated in patients with facial-proximal upper limb weakness or in affected FSHD probands’ relatives. We reviewed all cases molecularly investigated, evaluating phenotypes, indications for FSHD analysis, and alternative genetic diagnoses in D4Z4 alleles with ≥ 11 repeats carriersWe considered 506 patients referred to the Miogen Laboratory (University of Modena) in the last 15 years for 4q35 D4Z4 analysis. Of these, 357 (71%) carried a D4Z4-reduced allele (among them, 155 were symptomatic FSHD patients’ relatives): 63% displayed a classic facial-scapular phenotype (Clinical Category A), 11% B (scapular-girdle or orbicular involvement exclusively), 17% C (asymptomatic), and 9% D (with atypical clinical features).
Conversely, 149 (29%) carried D4Z4 alleles with ≥ 11 repeats excluding FSHD. Seventy-five of them (50.3%) were asymptomatic FSHD patients’ relativesAmong 55 sporadic symptomatic patients carrying D4Z4 alleles ≥ 11 repeats, 9 (16.6%) presented full phenotype (Category A), 3 (5.5%) were B, and 43 D (78.2%). ## Abstract
Facioscapulohumeral muscular dystrophy (FSHD) is a hereditary myopathy with a prevalence of 1:20.000. FSHD1 is associated with the reduction of the number of D4Z4 tandem repeats located at 4q35. FSHD2 classifies 5% of FSHD clinical cases with no D4Z4 reduced alleles. It has been reported that 80% of FSHD2 cases carry heterozygous mutation in the chromatin remodeling factor SMCHD1 required for normal DNA methylation levels and transcriptional repression. Pathogenic variants in SMCHD1 have been also causally associated with Bosma Syndrome and hypopituitarism. Considering this clinical heterogeneity, we collected aggregated data from whole genome, whole exome and sequencing panels provided by six centers from Italy, France and Finland. Three centers provide diagnosis for muscle genetic diseases and three centers investigate genetic syndromes and monogenic diseases. We evaluated sequencing data from 22.370 samples and identified 64 pathogenic SMCHD1. To functionally investigate these variants, we analyzed the D4Z4 methylation status as a read out of protein function. DNA samples from 22 subjects carrying 18 variants underwent methyl-seq analysis using a customized molecular and bioinformatic approach. In 9 samples we observed reduced D4Z4 methylation. Of these, 8 were from the myopathic cohorts and 1 from the cohorts including other phenotypes. Our analytical approach demonstrates that the reuse of genomic data from ample cohorts may support a more precise understanding of gene variants and offers the opportunity of preventing biased interpretation. This underlines that caution should be paid in the study of genotype-phenotype correlation and interpretation of gene variants effect.

LabellaB RizzardiA Cotti PiccinelliS ZattiC HansenC RomijndersR MaetzlerW CariaF RisiB DamioliS OlivieriE FerulloL PoliL PadovaniA PilottoA FilostoM 1 Department of Clinical and Experimental Sciences, University of Brescia, Italy2 Department of Continuity of Care and Frailty, Unit of Neurology, ASST Spedali Civili, Brescia, Italy3 Laboratory of digital Neurology and biosensors, University of Brescia, Italy4

## Abstract
The sensitivity of standard assessment of motor performance for evaluating progression and response to treatment in Late Onset Pompe Disease (LOPD) in clinical practice and clinical trials is still a controversial issue.
Mobile health technologies (MHT) have been demonstrated to improve diagnostic accuracy in several conditions with motor impairment by enabling detection of subtle motor dysfunctions.
Aim of our study was to test whether MHT could improve the evaluation of motor impairment in LOPD patientsSix LOPD patients who were able to walk without aids and treated with enzyme replacement therapy (ERT) were enrolled. They were divided into two groups: a group of younger patients who have short disease duration and few symptoms (Walton & Gardner-Medwin [WGM] scale 0; n = 3, age < 30 years old) and a group of older patients with longer disease duration and more evident clinical signs (Walton & Gardner-Medwin [WGM] scale range 2-3; n = 3, age 50-65 years old)Six minute walking test (6MWT), normal and fast “Timed Up and Go Test” (TUG) and speed in one time and repeated rising to chair were evaluated in all the patients by MHT-based gait analysis (Rehagait, Hasomed). Data were compared to age-matched healthy controls (HC, n = 41) using Mann–Whitney U testIn younger group, no significant differences in distance walked over 6 minutes and in time spent on TUG and rising from chair were observed compared with age-matched HC.
Differently, a significant inferior number of steps and a longer step and swing time were observed in patients than controls in 6MWT by MHT-based gait analysis.
MHTdid not show differences between patients and HC in raising from chair and TUG test.
In older group, longer time in raising from chair and TUG and in most of the variables measured by MHT during 6MWT (such as step count, step time, swing time, step time variability) was observed compared to HC as expectedOur preliminary results suggest that wearable technologies can identify subtle walking abnormalities in paucisymptomatic patients not otherwise evident by usual clinical evaluationThese findings may have important implications for management, follow-up and treatment decisions in clinical practice. Importantly, our results suggest that MHT deserve to be evaluated as promising outcome measures for clinical trials. Further studies in a larger population are warranted to confirm our findings.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolMitochondrial involvement in patients with autism spectrum disorders and intellectual disability: a histological and genetic study
ScuderiC Santa PaolaS Lo GiudiceM GiulianoM Di BlasiFD CittàS PettinatoR VitelloG A RomanoC BorgioneE 1EOasi Research Institute – IRCCS, Troina, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Autism spectrum disorders (ASD) are neurodevelopmental disorders, with heterogeneous aetiology, characterized by deficiencies in social interaction, communication, and by repetitive and stereotyped behaviours.
ASD has been associated with mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency and/or mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) mutations. However, few studies carried out detailed clinical, instrumental, morphological, biochemical investigations, and mtDNA sequencing in ASD patients.
Our study pointed at the identification of mitochondrial dysfunction in 19 selected subjects presenting ASD and clinical features suggestive of mitochondrial disease. For these patients we adopted a multidisciplinary approach combining clinical and laboratory investigations on muscle biopsy, with biochemical, histological and genetic techniquesThe histological examination showed myogenic or neurogenic changes in 79% of patients; furthermore, 58% of the patients had lipid accumulation, mitochondrial proliferation and COX deficient fibers. The biochemical investigations reported in three patients impairments involving one or more of the respiratory chain complexes. In addition, genetic studies revealed multiple mtDNA deletions in one patient, mtDNA point mutations in four patients (m.4320C > T in tRNAIle, m.8836A > G in ATP6, m.12235T > A in tRNASer(AGY), m.1683C > T in RNR2) and the common POLG1 pathogenic mutations T251I and P587L in cis in another oneThe present study confirms the hypothesis of an association between ASD and mitochondrial dysfunction. The high number of positive results reported in our study strengths the sampling criteria used by us for muscle biopsy and genetics studies, such as muscle tone alterations, weakness, high CK and/or lactate levels, abnormal EEG and/or EMG, white matter anomalies and/or cerebral atrophy.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolPregnancy in late-onset Pompe disease (LOPD) women: a multi-centre experience
MusumeciO VercelliL PorcinoM GadaletaG ArenaIG MonginiT RodolicoC ToscanoA 1Department of Clinical and experimental Medicine, University of Messina, Messina Italy1 Neuromuscular Unit, Department of Neuroscience RLM, University of Torino, Torino, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Late-onset Pompe disease (LOPD) is characterized by limb-girdle muscular weakness and respiratory insufficiency, which may affect pregnancy. A few reports suggested that motor and respiratory functions worsen during the gestational period, followed by postpartum complete recovery.
To investigate the pregnancy history in a cohort of LOPD females, focusing on fertility, obstetric history and disease course during pregnancies.
We collected data from 22 LOPD females followed in our Units through a questionnaire created in collaboration with gynecologists. The survey included information on age at pregnancy, number of pregnancies, pregnancy outcome, labour and postpartum complications and morbidities in the newborns.
Median age at LOPD diagnosis was 48 years (mean 42.1); median age at first pregnancy was 24.1 years (mean 23,5). 16/22 patients became pregnant prior to the occurrence of symptoms; a patient was diagnosed during her first pregnancy and three patients had at least a pregnancy after symptoms onset. 42 pregnancies were reported by 17 women (38 deliveries and 4 miscarriages) with a median of 2 pregnancies (range 0-4). Six women were nulliparae (median age 25 years). During the gestational period, two patients developed transient hypertransaminasemia, that normalized postpartum. All women have vaginal deliveries but two who had Caesarean section. One childbirth was complicated by retained placenta and postpartum haemorrhage. All patients started ERT years after their pregnancies.
Data collected in our cohort showed that clinical course and pregnancy outcome were mostly uneventful. The fertility does not seem to be affected, although some patients were nulliparae, may be due to the young age. Gestation and childbirth do not seem to accelerate or uncover latent LOPD.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolPrevalence of Sleep Disorders in a cohort of patients with Primary Mitochondrial Diseases
ArenaIG ConsuloC PorcinoM UsbergoC RodolicoC ToscanoA MusumeciO 1Unit of Neurology and Neuromuscular Disorders, Department of Clinical and Experimental Medicine, University of Messina, Messina, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract

## Introduction
Primary mitochondrial disorders (PMDs) are a heterogeneous group of diseases secondary to a mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation deficiency. The burden of sleep disorders (SDs) in PMDs has been recently studied with a prevalence of sleep-respiratory-related disorders (SRDs). Nevertheless, general assessment of PMDs does not often include a screening evaluation for these disturbances. The aim of our study was to evaluate the prevalence of SDs, in a cohort of PMD patients, using the common standard tool used for screening SDs in the general population.

## Methods
A structural sleep interview concerning insomnia, circadian sleep disorders and excessive diurnal sleepiness (EDS) was administered to 65 patients affected by PMDs and 70 controls. The Epworth-Sleepiness Scale (ESS) questionnaire was administered to those reporting EDS. Patients were investigated by RLS diagnostic interview. Respiratory assessment included Forced-Vital-Capacity (FVC), Cough-Peak-Expiratory-Flow (CPEF), arterial blood gases, and nocturnal oximetry. Quality of life was investigated by QoL questionnaire. Additionally, NMDAS and MFIS were performed.

## Results
26/65 presented a SD. The most frequently observed SDs were SRDs (60%) and chronic insomnia (30%), excessive daytime sleepiness, and periodic limb movements. Obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) was more frequent in patients with severe muscle involvement, while insomnia was more reported in patients affected by psychiatric comorbidities

## Conclusion
Our study confirms the higher prevalence of SDs in PMDs compared to general population. A complete sleep interview comprising the administration of routinary scales should represent the primary approach to eventually address clinician to further investigations and a correct management.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolClinico-pathologic features and molecular genetic spectrum in a cohort of adult patients with mtDNA maintenance disorders
MeneriM PigaD MagriF VelardoD CiscatoP ZanottiS SciaccoM ComiG:P: RonchiD 1
IRCCS Fondazione Ca’ Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Neurology Unit, Milan, Italy2 Dino Ferrari Center, Department of Pathophysiology and Transplantation, University of Milan, Milan, Italy3
IRCCS Fondazione Ca’ Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Neuromuscular and Rare Disease Unit, Milan, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) maintenance disorders are heterogenous clinical presentations ranging from infantile/pediatric syndromes to late-onset myopathic presentations with or without the extra muscular involvement. Since causative mutations are found in nuclear genes encoding for factors handling mtDNA replication and repair, these disorders are inherited as Mendelian traits.
In this study we aim to review clinical, histological and molecular features of a retrospective cohort of 152 adult patients (ratio F/M = 1.08) affected with mtDNA maintenance disorders and evaluated in our Center in the last 30 years.
Prominent muscle involvement was confirmed by the presence of eyelid ptosis (67%), muscle weakness (60%) and progressive external ophthalmoplegia (51%) in our cohort. Peripheral neuropathy was observed in 20% of cases. Neurosensorial hearing loss, dysphagia and dysphonia/rhinolalia were reported in 20%, 18% and 15% of patients. Mental retardation/dementia, cerebellar involvement and presence of extrapyramidal signs and symptoms were also observed.
The accumulation of mtDNA deletions or reduced mtDNA content was documented in the available 134 muscle samples by two independent techniques (i.e. Southern blot and PCR analysis). COX-negative fibers, with or without ragged-red fibers, were observed in all the muscle samples analyzed.
A molecular diagnosis was achieved in 109 subjects (72% of patients) from 86 independent families. Most represented genes are POLG (37%), TWNK (19%) and ANT1 (12%) followed by genes involved in dNTPs metabolism (TK2, DGUOK, TYMP) accounting for 13 cases. Genes identified in the last 10 years (DNA2, MGME1, RNASEH1, ENDOG) increased the diagnostic yield of 13%.

BuchignaniB SansoneF MarinellaG AstreaG FrosiniS Denise CassandriniD TonacciA ConteR BattiniR RubegniA SantorelliFM the InGene 2.0 group1 Department of Developmental Neuroscience, IRCCS Stella Maris, Calambrone, Pisa, Italy2 Ph.D. Programme in Clinical and Translational Sciences, University of Pisa, Pisa, Italy3

## Abstract
InGene 2.0 is an ICT-based research project aimed at integrating routine data collected in clinical, instrumental, and laboratory practice of neuromuscular diseases. Moreover, it can be supportive in defining personalized care protocols in adults and children with muscular disorders. The aim of this study is to describe the standardized follow-up of patients with isolated or minimally symptomatic hyperCKemiaWe enrolled all the patients who were referred to IRCCS Fondazione Stella Maris from August 2020 to January 2023 and matching the inclusion criteria. Patients underwent to exhaustive NGS multigene panel for myopathies. All the patients were assessed at baseline and monitored at 1-year follow-up with neurological examination, MRC scoring, and functional motor scales (NSAA and 6MWT).
About 35% of the110 patients recorded in InGene2.0 presented with isolated hyperCKemia. In this group, there were more adults than children. Segmental muscle strength was, in the majority of the cases, preserved. The values obtained in NSAA and 6MWT did not suggest muscle involvement at either baseline or follow-up. Almost 85% of the patients obtained a genetic diagnosis, including pathogenic/likely pathogenic variants in the RYR1 and MYH7 genesIn this study, an ICT-based platform facilitated the collection and storage of data for standardized follow-up in patients with isolated or minimally symptomatic hyperCKemia. Although most of the patients received a genetic diagnosis, the majority remained stable at 1-year follow-up.
The present work was done thanks to the project InGene 2.0, funded by Regione Toscana (Bando Salute 2018).

Acta MyologicaActa MyolLamotrigine as an anti-myotonic agent in myotonic dystrophy type 1: an open-label single-center pilot study
Cotti PiccinelliS RisiB CariaF DamioliS BertellaE GarofaliF Ait AllaliN LabellaB PoliL BonitoV PadovaniA FilostoM 1

## Abstract
Myotonic dystrophy type 1 (MD1) is an autosomal dominant multisystem condition that mainly causes progressive muscle loss, weakness and myotonia, caused by an abnormal expansion of a repeated region in the DMPK gene.
Myotonia, defined as the impairment of relaxation of skeletal muscles after voluntary contraction or electrical stimulation, is one of the core features of MD1 and can be very disabling for the patient by seriously interfering with the activities of daily lifeThe most used anti-myotonic drug is mexiletine which, however, is expensive and not always effective, has limited availability and several side effectsLamotrigine, an anti-epileptic drug that decrease the voltage-sensitive sodium channel activity, unlike mexiletine, is available, inexpensive and well tolerated and showed efficacy in treating myotonia in in-vitro model and in patients affected with non-dystrophic myotoniaOn this basis, we here investigated the potential of using lamotrigine for treatment of myotonia in patients with dystrophic myotoniasIn this open label pilot study, we included adult patients with clinical myotonia affecting daily living and genetically confirmed MD1. All patients received lamotrigine at increasing dosage starting from 50 mg/daily up to 200 mg/daily. The primary outcome was a severity score of myotonia, the Myotonic Behaviour Scale (MPS) ranging from asymptomatic (score 1) to invalidating myotonia (score 6) reported by the participants.
Clinical myotonia, 9-Hole Peg Test (9HPT) and SF-36 questionnaire were also assessedWe enrolled 12 DM1 patient. Medium age at enrolment was 43 and latest follow-up available was after 4 months (1 to 10, median 3). Medium MPS score at baseline was 3.22 while at last follow-up was 2.43 (p < 0.05). At baseline medium 9HPT time was 34,7 seconds while at last follow-up was 29.8 (p < 0.05). No significant adverse events were recordedOur pilot study shows very encouraging findings on using lamotrigine as an anti-myotonic agent in DM1 patients and should lead to confirm these results on a large scale in controlled studies.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolTherapeutic approaches to adult Ab anti-NXP2+ Dermatomyositis: a single center experience
UrbanoG GadaletaG RumboloF MengozziG BoschiS Chiadò-PiatL RolleE VercelliE MonginiT 1Neuromuscular Unit, Department of Neurosciences Rita Levi Montalcini, University of Torino, Italy1 SC Biochimica Clinica, AOU Città della Salute e della Scienza di Torino162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. The article can be used by giving appropriate credit and mentioning the license, but only for non-commercial purposes and only in the original version. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Dermatomyositis is a rare neuromuscular disease belonging to the group of idiopathic inflammatory myopathies. The discovery of myositis-specific antibodies (MSA) permitted to distinguish clinical phenotypes and raised awareness of the necessity to tailor follow-up and therapeutic approaches: among these antibodies, anti-nuclear matrix protein 2 (Ab anti-NXP2) emerge for the aggressiveness of the muscular and cutaneous involvement and the increased malignancy risk. Our cohort was composed of nine cases (2 males and 7 females) of adult Ab anti-NXP2+ dermatomyositis: the median age of onset was 56,2 years and the clinical presentation was represented by proximal weakness (100%), pathognomonic or characteristic cutaneous signs (89%) and elevated Creatin Kinase (mean level 5008 Ui/L). None presented cardiological symptoms and only 3 patients (33%) had mild respiratory involvement, while dysphagia was frequent (55%), requiring positioning of a nasogastric tube (33%) and percutaneous endoscopic gastrotomy (11%). Seven patients (78%) necessitated hospitalization and one patient (11%) died. Malignancies have not been detected in our cohort. Despite all patients undergoing high doses of corticosteroids with subsequent decalage and at least one immunomodulant or immunosuppressive therapy -methotrexate, hydroxychloroquine, azathioprine, intravenous immunoglobulins, and plasmapheresis-, it was not sufficient to avoid relapses that happened to five patients (55%). Rituximab was indicated as an off-label therapy for five patients (55%) with a positive impact on muscular and skin involvement. In the absence of univocal guidelines, our experience suggests a “Hit Early, Hit Hard” pharmacological strategy and a close follow-up to detect possible relapses.

## Abstract
Granulomatous myositis (GM) is a rare condition whose precise clinical features and prognosis have not yet been well defined. We retrospectively examined the clinical, biological and histopathological data, along with treatment response of all patients who referred to the Groupe Hospitalier Pitié-Salpêtrière with myositis and non-caseating granuloma on muscle biopsy.
23 patients were found to have GM, who could be subdivided into 3 main groups: 13 patients with GM and systemic sarcoidosis (6 of whom subsequently developed probable/defined sIBM), 7 patients with idiopathic isolated GM (2 of whom developed also sIBM), 3 patients with GM and Crohn’s disease.
Patients with GM and pure sarcoidosis, as well as patients with isolated GM, exhibited variable clinical features ranging from myalgias to mostly symmetrical proximo-distal weakness of four limbs whereas patients with GM associated with Crohn’s disease presented only mild clinical impairment and good outcome. Conversely, patients with GM and sIBM showed more severe asymmetric muscle weakness, as well as an increased occurrence of dysphagia and poor treatment responseAs already described, a frequent association of GM with sIBM and/or sarcoidosis is confirmed.
When associated with sIBM, GM will lead to a more severe clinical impairment and worst outcome. On the other hand, isolated GM, GM-Sarcoidosis and GM-Chron’s disease association show a satisfactory treatment response along with a good clinical outcome in most cases.

## Abstract
Idiopathic inflammatory myopathies(IIMs) represent a heterogeneous disease group characterized by proximal muscle weakness, and inflammatory findings on muscle biopsy. Main IIMs subtypes are dermatomyositis(DM), immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy(IMNM), inclusion body myositis(sIBM), and polymyositis. In our study we evaluated a panel of inflammatory biomarkers, mainly including cytokines, in the serum of IIMsWe measured the concentration of APRIL, BAFF, CHI3L1, Osteopontin, IL-6, IL-8, CCL2, CXCL10, CXCL12 and CXCL-13 through a Luminex Assay in the serum of patients with different IIM subtypes and a healthy subjects’ control group. Eventual between groups differences were evaluated through ANOVA test with Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisonWe enrolled 41 subjects, 34 IIMs patients (9 INMM, 15 DM, and 10 sIBM) and 7 healthy subjectsCHI3L1 levels were significantly higher in INMN and s-IBM groups compared to controlsIL8, CXCL10 and CXCL13 were higher in all IIMs groups compared to healthy subjects with the highest levels of CXCL10 in sIBM, and of CXCL13 in sIBM and DM. CXCL12 levels were lower in DM patients compared to all the other groups. Ostopontin levels resulted higher in NAM and DM patients compared to control group. We did not find significant differences between groups for APRIL, BAFF, CCL-2, and IL-6 serum levelsIn our study we found a significant elevation of several inflammatory biomarkers in the serum of IIMs patients compared to healthy subjects. Moreover, different IIMs subtypes presented specific biomarkers’ signature, as for CXCL10 specifically elevated in the serum of sIBM patients.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolClinical and functional characterization of the novel Nav1.4 sodium channel mutation, p.T592I, found in a three-generation family
CampanaleC LaghettiP SaltarellaI AltamuraC BernasconiP MantegazzaR BrugnoniR TacconiP DesaphyJF 1 Section of Pharmacology, Department of Precision and Regenerative Medicine, School of Medicine, University of Bari Aldo Moro, Bari, Italy2

## Abstract
A novel heterozygous point mutation (c.1775C > T, p.Thr592Ile) in the muscle voltage-gated sodium channel α subunit gene (SCN4A) has been identified in five female patients over three generations, in a family with non-dystrophic myotonia. Muscle stiffness appearing early after birth involves mainly the face and hands, but also affects walking and running later. It presents a clear cold sensitivity; very hot temperatures, menstruation and pregnancy also exacerbate the symptoms; muscle pain and a warm-up phenomenon are variable features. Neither paralytic attacks nor post-exercise weakness has been reported. The symptoms were controlled with mexiletine or acetazolamide. The Short Exercise test after muscle cooling revealed two different EMG patterns, with moderate absolute changes of compound muscle action potential amplitudeThe T592I mutation was introduced in SCN4A cDNA for heterologous expression in HEK cells. Wild-type and T592I sodium currents were recorded using whole-cell patch-clamp technique in transfected HEK293 cells. The T592I and WT sodium currents were very similar, showing rapid activation and fast inactivation. No difference was found in voltage dependence of fast and slow inactivation. Yet, the voltage-dependence of T592I activation was significantly negatively shifted by ~5 mV, thereby increasing the likelihood of the channel to open at negative potentials. Such effect may likely cause the muscle stiffness. (Supported by University of Bari “Horizon Europe Seeds” - project Medineuropa).

Acta MyologicaActa MyolPhenotypic variability of Andersen–Tawil Syndrome in a family sharing the same allelic KCNJ2 gene mutation
OnoreME PicilloE TorellaA CesaroneE PilusoG NigroV PolitanoL 1 Department of Precision Medicine, University Hospital and University of Campania 'Luigi Vanvitelli', Naples, Italy2
Telethon Institute of Genetics and Medicine, Pozzuoli (NA)3 Cardiomyology and Medical Genetics, University Hospital and University of Campania 'Luigi Vanvitelli', Naples, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Andersen–Tawil Syndrome (ATS) also defined as Andersen Cardiodysrhythmic Periodic Paralysis, is a multisystem channelopathy characterized by periodic paralysis, ventricular arrhythmias, prolonged QT interval, and facial dysmorphisms. Advances in NGS technologies in recent years have allowed the identification of a much larger number of cases, highlighting the high phenotypic variability and incomplete penetrance of this condition.
We describe a two-generation family with six individuals affected by ATS with a broad spectrum of phenotypic presentations. The proband is a 37-year-old woman who presented episodes of muscle weakness and cramps, since the age of 16. Her father and two brothers complained similar symptoms. Treatment with magnesium and carbonic anhydrase inhibitors was of benefit, suggesting a diagnosis of channelopathy. WES analysis detected a heterozygous pathogenic variant (NM_000891.2: c.652C > T, p. Arg218Trp) in KCNJ2 gene related to ATS, encoding the inwardly rectifying potassium channel Kir2.1. In physiological conditions, Kir2.1 channels are involved in the regulation of excitability and the maintenance of the resting membrane potential of different tissues, including the heart and skeletal muscle. The mutation has a known loss of function and dominant-negative effect on KCNJ2 current. The segregation studies, performed using Sanger sequencing, confirmed the presence of the variant in all affected family members. The genetic background and other non-genetic factors may modify the course of diseases. This report suggests that many cases may remain underdiagnosed due to a less severe phenotype. WES/WGS screenings can highlight KCNJ2 gene variability and help gain a more accurate view of the genotype-phenotype relationship.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolDystrophin and utrophin quantitation by targeted mass spectrometry in Duchenne and Becker muscular dystrophy
RiguzziP BelloL PenzoM VillaM CapeceG ZangaroV GorgoglioneD VianelloS SabbatiniD CanessaE HathoutY PegoraroE 1Department of Neurosciences, DNS, University of Padova, Italy1
School of Pharmacy and Pharmaceutical Sciences, Binghamton University (SUNY), Binghamton, NY162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. The article can be used by giving appropriate credit and mentioning the license, but only for non-commercial purposes and only in the original version. ## Abstract
The relationship between dystrophin and utrophin quantity and the severity of dystrophinopathy phenotypes is a complex and debated issue, with important therapeutic implications. The most common quantitation techniques, i.e. immunohistochemistry/immunofluorescence and Western blot, are substantially semi-quantitative in nature. Targeted mass spectrometry allows precise quantitation of proteins, using as control “heavy labeled” proteins from SILAC (stable isotope labeling by amino acid in cell culture) human myotubesWe used this method to quantitate dystrophin and utrophin in muscle biopsy samples of 67 male dystrophinopathy patients, of which 16 DMD (age 20.7±8.9 years), 27 “typical” BMD (age 43.5±18.6), and 24 “mild” BMD (age 36.8±13.4), the latter defined as those patients showing no clear muscle weakness at clinical examination with manual muscle testing and North Star Ambulatory AssessmentDystrophin quantity in the three categories was respectively: 0±0%, 46.3±26.2%, and 85.9±50.2%, showing an inverse correlation with clinical severity. Conversely, utrophin quantity was directly correlated with clinical severity, averaging in the three groups: 341.9±202.2%, 183.3±106.6%, and 111.1±71.2%. Looking at age at loss of ambulation, 12/17 patients with 0% dystrophin lost ambulation, at a median age of 12 years; only 1/8 patients with dystrophin 1~30% lost ambulation at age 18 years; 2/8 patients with dystrophin 31~50% lost ambulation at age 35 and 70 years; and 2/34 patients with dystrophin > 50% lost ambulation at age 41 and 54 years. Interestingly, 5 patients had dystrophin quantities above 100%, ranging up to 268% of controlWith a quantitative technique based on advanced proteomics with stable isotope labeled human myoblast controls, we confirm a linear (rather than threshold) inverse correlation between dystrophin quantity and the severity of dystrophinopathy in males, and a direct correlation with utrophin levels, which is upregulated in DMD and close to normal in mild BMD. A relevant effect on phenotype of small dystrophin quantities is also confirmed.

## Abstract
Becker Muscular Dystrophy (BMD) is a rare progressive neuromuscular disease caused by mutations in the DMD gene, encoding dystrophin protein. We collected retrospective data from an Italian Multicentre BMD cohort to describe the natural history of the disease.
Retrospective demographic, clinical and genetic information was collected from 856 BMD patients followed by 17 Italian neuromuscular centres. Statistical analyses were conducted to describe the cohort and to investigate the age at loss of ambulation (LoA) and cardiac and respiratory functionAnalysis of LoA in our BMD cohort (mean age 30.18; CI: 29.02-31.349) showed that mean age at LoA was 35.10 years (CI: 31.58-38.62) with median survival age at LoA of 69 years. At the last assessment 29.2% had a value of LVEF% < 55% indicative of cardiomyopathy and 2.17% FVC% < 50% indicative of respiratory impairment. We further grouped patients by reading frame retention of the DMD gene. The subset of patients carrying in-frame large deletions or duplications of the DMD gene was further classified based on the specific mutation. Out-of-frame mutations (9%) are associated with an earlier LoA (p < 0.001). In addition, by comparing the different mutational subgroups we observed that deletions 45-49 were associated to earlier LoA (p < 0.001), whereas N-terminal (p < 0.05) and 45-48 (p < 0.01) deletions to decreased left ventricular systolic functionOur preliminary results contribute to better define the natural history of the disease. The knowledge of the natural history in subsets of BMD patients carrying homogenous DMD mutation will serve as an in vivo model for the identification of successful therapies in DMD.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolRobust preclinical data support development of DYNE-251 as a potential treatment for individuals with DMD mutations amenable to exon 51 skipping
DelageE CotaliE NaylorML DesjardinsCA VenkatesanR O’DonnellE HallJ RussoR SpringS TangK DavisJWII WeedenT ZanottiS MixC HanB BeskrovnayaO DugarA 1Dyne Therapeutics Inc., Waltham, MA, USA162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD) is characterized by progressive loss of muscle function leading to premature death. Current therapeutic approaches use exon skipping phosphorodiamidate morpholino oligomers (PMOs) that enable the translation of a shortened, functional dystrophin protein, but their success has been hampered by poor muscle delivery. We developed the FORCE™ platform, which harnesses the natural expression of transferrin receptor (TfR)1 on muscle cells for targeted delivery of oligonucleotides. DYNE-251, an investigational therapeutic for the treatment of DMD, consists of an exon 51 skipping PMO conjugated to an antigen-binding fragment (Fab) targeting TfR1. In non-human primates, DYNE-251 led to pronounced exon skipping in cardiac and skeletal muscle and demonstrated a favorable safety profile. We also demonstrated that a single dose of FORCE-M23D, a mouse-specific Fab-PMO conjugate designed to skip exon 23 of the murine Dmd pre-mRNA, led to robust and durable exon skipping and dystrophin restoration, and improved functional outcomes in mdx mice, compared to unconjugated PMO. These preclinical data supported the initiation of DELIVER, a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multiple ascending dose (MAD) study of DYNE-251 administered intravenously to ~46 ambulant and non-ambulant males (4-16 years) with exon 51 skip-amenable mutations (NCT05524883). The purpose of the study is to evaluate the safety, tolerability, and dystrophin levels in muscle following multiple doses of DYNE-251. Primary outcomes are the number of participants with treatment-emergent adverse events and the change from baseline in dystrophin levels in muscle at Week 25. The DELIVER study will inform the potential of DYNE-251 as a treatment for DMD.

## Abstract
Diaphragmatic sleep disordered breathing (dSDB) has been recently identified as sleep dysfunction secondary to diaphragmatic weakness in Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy (DMD). However, scoring criteria for the identification of dSDB are missing.
This study aimed to define and validate dSDB scoring criteria and to evaluate whether dSDB severity correlates with DMD respiratory progression.
Scoring criteria for diaphragmatic apnoea (dA) and hypopnoeas (dH) have been defined by the authors considering the pattern observed on cardiorespiratory polygraphy (CR) and the pathophysiology of dSDB.
Ten sleep professionals (physiologists, consultants) blinded to each other were involved in a three-round Delphi survey to rate each item of the proposed dSDB criteria (Likert scale 1-5) and to recognise dSDB amongst other SDB. Scorers’ accuracy was tested against the authors’ panel.
CR previously conducted in DMD were re-scored and a dAHI was derived. Pulmonary function (FVC%pred) and overnight SpO2 and tcCO2 were correlated with dAHIAfter the second round of Delphi, scorers deemed each item of dA and dH criteria as relevant as 4 or 5. The agreement with the panel in recognising dSDB was 81%, kappa 0.71, sensitivity 77%, specificity 85%. Thirty-two CR from DMD patients were reviewed. dSDB was previously scored as obstructive. The dAHI negatively correlated with FVC%pred (r-4;p < 0.05). Total number of dA positively correlated with mean overnight tcCO2 (r 0.4;p < 0.05).
Diaphragmatic SDB is a newly defined sleep disorder that correlates with DMD progression. A broader, prospective study to evaluate dSDB as a novel respiratory measure for DMD in clinical and research settings is planned.
