## Abstract
Facioscapulohumeral muscular dystrophy is one of the most common forms in adult patients and is characterized by extreme clinical variability, with genotype-phenotype scarce correlation in many cases. Moreover, to date physiopathology has not been clearly understood. In the last years, growing evidence proposed by many groups worldwide have started proposing new potential therapeutic targets and clinical trials are approaching. To avoid incorrect selection of patients and misinterpretation of trials’ results, the FSHD scientific community and pharmaceutical companies must achieve a clearer understanding of the phenotype differences among patients and share common evaluation standards and outcome measures. We present a four-year retrospective clinical follow-up from 41 FSHD patients along with qualitative muscle MRI of lower limbs of 36. We assessed demographic data, D4Z4 allele dimension, Clinical Categories, Clinical Severity Scoring (CSS) and the Ricci score and evaluated correlation and variations among those parameters. We also evaluated fatty infiltration at muscle MRI through Mercuri scale. Importantly, we found a more severe disease form in patients of category A compared to B at baseline and in terms of progression in the CSS, while patients did not progress according to the Ricci score. Muscle MRI evidenced differences in the involvement pattern and severity among patients of category A and B, although wider studies are needed to confirm our data. In conclusion, common effort is needed to identify stronger outcome measures to use in clinical trials.

MinacapilliE DosiC ZaninR RicciF GibertiniS MaggiL MassonR 1 Developmental Neurology Unit, Department of Paediatric Neurosciences, Fondazione IRCCS Istituto Neurologico Carlo Besta, Milan, Italy2 Department of Biomedical and Clinical Sciences, Postgraduate School of Child Neuropsychiatry, University of Milan, Milan, Italy3 Child Neuropsychiatry Unit, AO Città della Salute e della Scienza della Città di Torino4 Muscle Cell Biology Laboratory, Neuroimmunology and Neuromuscular Diseases Unit, Fondazione IRCCS Istituto Neurologico Carlo Besta, Milan, Italy5 Neuroimmunology and Neuromuscular Diseases Unit, Fondazione IRCCS Istituto Neurologico Carlo Besta, Milan, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Mutations in the LMNA gene are the main cause of laminopathies. Emery-Dreifuss muscular dystrophy (EDMD), limb-girdle muscular dystrophy type 1B, dilated cardiomyopathy with conduction system abnormalities and arrhythmias (DCMCD), and congenital form of muscular dystrophy (L-CMD) are among the laminopathies affecting striated muscle.
We describe a four-year-old boy carrying a novel heterozygous LMNA mutation (exon 8: c.1403A > C_p.Q468P), showing a phenotypic overlap between Emery-Dreifuss muscular dystrophy (EDMD) and LMNA-related congenital muscular dystrophy (LCMD): early and severe Achilles tendon contractures, initial elbow retraction, limb-girdle weakness, decreased strength of the extensor and flexor muscles of the head. The most prominent sign at the onset was bilateral retraction of the Achilles tendon. Muscle weakness mainly affected girdles and axis. Muscle biopsy showed neuro-myogenic pathological changes in muscle tissue, with modest degenerative-regenerative signs. NGS custom target gene testing for muscular dystrophies and congenital myopathies detected a never reported, de novo, likely pathogenic LMNA mutation, confirming the diagnosis of laminopathy. Severe Achilles tendon contractures required lengthening surgery with a positive outcome, as documented by standardized motor assessment. Considering the overall stability of muscle involvement and poor inflammatory finding at muscle biopsy, we decided to postpone corticosteroid treatment, in a wait-and-see approach.
This case suggests that early and severe Achilles tendon contractures, even if associated with a non-typical phenotype, should prompt consideration of LMNA gene sequencing. Surgical treatment can be functionally important and corticosteroid treatment may be considered.

RimoldiM MagriF AntognozziS RomagnoliG VelardoD ParenteV CinnanteC CortiS ComiGP Dario RonchiD 1

## Abstract
Biallelic mutations in TRIM32, encoding the Tripartite Motif-Containing Protein 32, cause a rare form of Limb Girdle Muscular Dystrophy (LGMDR8).
Most of patients so far reported belong to Hutterite population of Manitoba and exhibit distinct clinical features such as proximal muscle weakness, respiratory involvement, chronic keratitis, mild ankle contractions, and calf pseudohypertrophy. More recently, the identification of novel mutations in different ethnic backgrounds has further expanded the clinical phenotype of this disorder.
Here we describe the clinical, histopathological and the muscle magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) data of a 63-years-old Italian man who experienced progressive muscle weakness and hyperCKemia (> 3000 U/L) since the age of 39 years, presenting the TRIM32 variants c.1181G > A (p.Arg394His) and c.1781G > A, (p. Ser594Asp).
The patient presented severe weakness at lower limbs conditioning waddling gait with bilateral foot drop associated to hypotrophy of the anterior compartment of the thigh and of the gastrocnemius bilaterally. At upper limbs pectoral muscle was selectively involved and winged scapula were present. Cranial nerves were preserved. Cardiac and respiratory involvement was absent. Electromyography showed myopatic changes.
Muscle biopsy showed mild fibroadipose replacement, predominance of type 2 fibers with size variability, splitting and vacuolization.
Few muscle MRI studies have been performed in TRIM32 subjects so far. In our patient, both upper and lower limbs showed severe fibroadipose degeneration, involving predominantly the scapular and pelvic girdle and the anterior compartment of the thigh with selective sparing of specific muscles.
Furthermore, we compared the findings in our case with those reported in other TRIM32-mutated patients.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA retrospective analisys of motor and respiratory progression in a population of FSHD1 patients: how fast does the disease go?
ColaciccoG CarraroE MartinelliL De MattiaE RomaE CrociM LizioA SansoneV A 1The NeMO Clinical Center, Neurorehabilitation Unit, University of Milan162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
In FSHD weakness typically affects face, upper limb girdle, and eventually lower limbs, with a wide range of severity. Respiratory involvement is described as mild and occurring late in the course of the disease.
To investigate 5-year change in motor and respiratory progression in ambulant FSHD1 patients, we collected motor [10Meter (10MWT); 6minute walking test (6MWT)] and respiratory data (Forced Vital Capacity, FVC) from genetically confirmed patients with FSHD1, aged ≥ 18.
Out of 69, a total of 35 FSHD1 patients with at least one follow-up visit, were retrospectively recruited (mean age at first evaluation: 39.45 years ± 14.12; male/female ratio: 1.69; mean follow-up period: 44.99 months ± 16.17). Both motor and respiratory assessments significantly changed over-time; however, the Cohen’s d effect size of changes was in a range between small to moderate, except for the 10MWT, which reported a moderate to large effect size. Changes in FVC % in both seated and supine position significantly correlated with changes in 10MWT (r = -0.36, p = 0.0463; r = -0.43, p = 0.0187; respectively) and 6MWT (r = 0.50, p = 0.0033; r = 0.37, p = 0.0455; respectively).
Our data demonstrate a correlation between motor and respiratory progression, although the statistically significant changes in % FVC were not clinically meaningful, confirming the mild respiratory involvement in FSHD. The moderate-large reduction in 10MWT is a good indicator of motor involvement. The reduction in 6MWT scores is mild probably influenced not only by the motor and respiratory involvement but also by fatigue and endurance.

## Abstract
Dystroglycanopathies are muscle disorders due to abnormal glycosylation of dystroglycan which is a cell-surface glycoprotein acting as a receptor for extracellular matrix proteins containing laminin-G domains. They are clinically and genetically heterogeneous and encompass a wide spectrum of phenotypic severity, including adult-onset Limb-girdle Muscular Dystrophy 19 and congenital muscular-dystrophies.
Mutations in the guanosine diphosphate mannose (GDP-mannose) pyrophosphorylase B (GMPPB) gene are unfrequently reported in patients with dystroglycanopathy.
A 15-year-old boy (patient 1) presented with mild proximal weakness and myalgia at the lower limbs since age of 4 years. His 7-years-old sister (patient 2) had increase value of Creatine Kinase (CK) (1200 U/L). No cognitive or other system involvement was reported. Parents were not consanguineous.
A neurophysiological, pathological and genetic assessment was performed.
Patient 1 neurological examination disclosed moderate proximal weakness. EMG showed proximal myogenic findings. Muscle MRI displayed increased subcutaneous adipose tissue and initial fat replacement in lower limbs. Muscle biopsy detected multifocal endomysial fibrosis, scattered atrophic fibers, some hypertrophic fibers, fibers with internal nuclei and phagocytosis.
Patient 2 neurological examination revealed only mild proximal weakness in the lower limbs. Targeted next generation sequencing disclosed the homozygote missense c.458C > T, p. Thr153Ile change in the GMPPB gene in both patients, which was not reported in LOVD and with prediction of disease-causing mutation.
In this study, we reported the occurrence of a novel homozygote mutation associated with a mild rare LGMDR19 phenotype. Our results emphasize the allelic and phenotypic heterogeneity of the GMPPB-linked disease and should lead to evaluate this gene in the diagnostic process of isolated hyperCKemia and mild forms of proximal myopathy.

## Abstract
A 51 y.o. male patient, with non consanguineous parents and negative familiar history came to our department complaining of progressive muscle weakness at four limbs. Motor impairment began at 30 y.o. after a femoral fracture, while arms weakness progressively developed in the last 5 years. Neurologic examination evidenced weakness on deltoids (MRC 4/4), pectorals (3/3), quadriceps (3/3), glutei (2/2) and iliopsoas (4/4), marked calf pseudohypertrophy. Mild increased serum CK (about 500 IU/L) was found in multiple tests. Patient underwent an electromyography that showed a diffuse myopathic pattern without evidence of spontaneous activity. Suspecting a limb-girdle dystrophy an NGS panel was performed. Heterozygous pathogenic variant of CAPN3 gene was identified only in patient and not in his familiar, however, according to the European Neuromuscular Centre workshop criteria for Calpainopathy, due to unusual calf hypertrophy, an Xp21 MLPA test was executed, showing an in-frame mutation (deletion of exons 45 to 48). Patient was diagnosed with an overlapping syndrome of LGMDD4 and Becker muscular dystrophy. This diagnosis allows more complex speculations on the still actual role of clinical and histological examinations in the near era of multiple available gene therapies and prominent gene testing in neuromuscular disorders.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolLMGD-D2 TNPO3 related: A quality of life study
AngeliniC RodriguezAA 1 Campus Pietro D’Abano, University of Padova, Padova, Italy2 Neuro-e-Motion Research Team, Faculty of Health Sciences, Department of Psychology, University of Deusto, Bilbao, Spain162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
The present study is the first research that analyses the QoL of people with LGMD-D2. The aim was to assess the quality of life in patients with LGMD-D2 and to analyze the differences between patients’ reports and caregivers’ reports. The instruments used were as follows: sociodemographic data, GSGC and INQoL instrument. The sample consisted of 6 people affected by LGMD-D2 and 3 caregivers of 3 affected people, from associations of affected people and the hospital of Padua.
Those affected have multiple symptoms that lead to disability and dependence, which ultimately lead to dependence on their environment. In the current context of constantly developing research for new treatments, it is essential to analyze which aspects are most affected. The present study shows that LGMD-D2 has a greater impact on activities of daily living, independence, emotional sphere, and body image perception than other LGMD pathologies or other NMDs. It also appears that the impact is greater if the affected person is older, and that muscle weakness is a very disabling symptom in this variant. Finally, caregivers can be a very important people in symptom reporting, as certain psychological adjustment mechanisms in the patient may be interfering with the objectivity of the report.

## Abstract
Muscular dystrophies represent a heterogeneous group of genetic disorders associated with an aberrant inflammation which contributes to the pathogenesis of the disease by impairing regeneration and inducing fibrosis. Sarcoglycanopathies are recessive inherited limb-girdle muscular dystrophies (LGMDs) due to mutations in sarcoglycan genes, where the role of inflammation has been poorly investigated.
Aim of this study is to better characterize the local and peripheral inflammatory pattern of patients affected by alfa-sarcoglycanopahy (LGMDR3).
Bulk RNA sequencing of muscle biopsy was performed in 17 patients affected by LGMDR3, classified as severe or mild on the basis of clinical and genetical parameters, and 10 unaffected individuals. Unsupervised Pearson hierarchical clustering analysis of 5000 genes with the highest variance demonstrates that the gene expression profile of severe LGMDR3 is strongly different compared to controls samples, whereas mild LGMDR3 show no consistent differences to controls. The analysis of inflammation response genes reveals overexpression of innate immune system pathways and higher abundance of inflammatory infiltrate, mainly consisting of monocytes, cytotoxic T cells and myeloid dendritic cells, in severe LGMDR3 as compared to controls. Immunophenotypical analysis by flow cytometry of peripheral blood mononuclear cells indicates that the percentage of CD8, TH1 lymphocytes and activated monocytes are significantly increased in LGMDR3 patients in comparison to controls.
In conclusion, this study provides a clear characterization of muscular and peripheral immunological profiles of patients affected by LGMDR3 suggesting a relevant role of inflammatory responses in the pathogenesis of this muscular dystrophy.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA novel missense variant in the ACADVL gene in a patient with VLCAD deficiency: a clinical and genetic study
CariaF Cotti PiccinelliS DamioliS RisiB BertellaE BonitoV LabellaB PoliL PadovaniA FilostoM 1
NeMO-Brescia Clinical Center for Neuromuscular Diseases, Brescia, Italy2 Department of Clinical and Experimental Sciences, University of Brescia, Brescia, Italy3 Unit of Neurology, ASST “Spedali Civili”, Brescia, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Very long-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (VLCAD) deficiency is a rare autosomal recessive long chain fatty acid oxidation disorder caused by mutations in the ACADVL gene. The myopathic form presents with exercise intolerance, exercise-related rhabdomyolysis and muscle pain usually starting during adolescence or adulthood.
We report clinical and genetic findings of a VLCAD subject carrying a novel pathogenic mutation in ACADVL.
A 17-year-old boy presented with exercise-induced muscle pain and fatigue since his childhood. In the recent clinical history, episodes of exercise-related severe hyperCKemia and myoglobinuria were reported.
A neurophysiological, myopathological and biochemical assessment was carried out. A 34-gene panel NGS analysis for metabolic myopathies and a familial segregation analysis were conducted.
Electromyography and electroneurography were normal. Muscle biopsy showed “moth-eaten” fibers, and mild increase in lipid storage in muscle fibers. PAS staining was normal.
NGS analysis displayed the already known heterozygote c.1769G > A (p.Arg590Gln) variant and the unreported heterozygote c.523C > T (p.Gly175Arg) change in ACADVL both having disease causing prediction. Healthy mother and father were heterozygous for c.523T > C and c.1769G > A respectively.
Plasma acylcarnitine profile revealed high long chain acylcarnitine species levels, especially C14:1.
The very long-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase is an enzyme that catalyzes most of the dehydrogenation of mitochondrial palmitoyl-CoA in liver, heart, and skeletal muscle.
Clinical, histopathological, biochemical and genetic tests support the diagnosis of VLCAD deficiency in our patient. The report of a novel pathogenic missense variant in ACADVL expands the allelic heterogeneity of the disease.
VLCAD is a very rare metabolic myopathy; prompt diagnosis is essential for early starting a specific diet and avoiding prolonged fasting and strenuous exercise in order to minimize muscle damage and slow the disease progression.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolCardiopulmonary exercise testing as outcome measure in primary mitochondrial myopathy: comparison with other functional measures
ArenaIG UsbergoC ConsuloC RodolicoC ToscanoA MusumeciO 1Unit of Neurology and Neuromuscular Disorders, Department of Clinical and Experimental Medicine, University of Messina, Messina, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. The article can be used by giving appropriate credit and mentioning the license, but only for non-commercial purposes and only in the original version. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Primary mitochondrial myopathies (PMMs) are a group of oxidative phosphorylation disorders characterized by a mitochondrial dysfunction secondary to mutations of mtDNA or nDNA and presenting with a predominant but not exclusive skeletal muscle involvement. Different outcome measures have been evaluated in the assessment of exercise ability and motor performance in these patients. In this study, we explored the application of Cardiopulmonary Exercise Test (CPET) and other outcome measures in the assessment of exercise capacity, trying to compare them in PMM patients.
20 patients with PMM (including 12 with mtDNA mutation or single deletions and 8 with nDNA mutation) and 10 matched healthy controls were included. CPET was performed according to standard procedures and maximal oxygen uptake (VO2max), maximal workload (Wmax), ventilatory and cardiological responses were evaluated. Six-Minute-Walk-Test (6MWT), Triple Timed-Up-and-Go test (3TUG), Five Times Sit-to-Stand test (5XSST), spirometry, NMDAS were also carried out. A Spearman’s rank calculator was used for statistical analysis.
All patients showed a reduction in exercise capacity as evaluated by VO2max (mean 16.41, range 10.3-23.1), Wmax (mean 85.1, range 29-129). VO2max, Wmax were reduced compared to controls (p < 0.05). VO2max was correlated to 6MWT distance walked (DW) (p < 0.05).
CPET represents a performance outcome measure providing a more comprehensive assessment of exercise capacity, identifying specific exercise-limiting factors such as pulmonary, cardiovascular, or musculoskeletal limitations. Our preliminary findings showed a possible correlation between VO2max and DW on 6MWT in PMMs, suggesting the possibility to include CPET as outcome measure in the follow up of these patients.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA novel compound homozygous ACO2 mutation in an infant with fatal progressive encephalopathy: biochemical characterization on muscle tissue
SomàA RicciFS StangaS MezzanotteM MarinaccioC D’AlessandroR SottemanoS MoranaG SpadaM BoidoM MonginiTE 1 Division of Child and Adolescent Neuropsychiatry, Department of Public Health and Pediatric Sciences, University of Turin, Turin, Italy2 Department of Neuroscience “Rita Levi Montalcini”, Neuroscience Institute Cavalieri Ottolenghi, University of Turin, Turin, Italy3 Division of Child and Adolescent Neuropsychiatry, Department of Child Care and Pathology, Children Hospital “Regina Margherita”,

For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
The ACO2 gene encodes the mitochondrial protein aconitate hydratase. Mitochondrial aconitase is ubiquitously expressed and catalyses the interconversion of citrate into isocitrate in the tricarboxylic acid (TCA) cycle. Deficiencies in the TCA-cycle enzymes have been reported to cause various neurodegenerative diseases, due to disruption of cellular energy metabolism and development of oxidative stress.
We investigated a severe early infantile-onset neurometabolic syndrome due to a homozygous novel compound variant in exon 13 of the ACO2 gene. The in vitro pathogenicity of this variant of unknown significance was demonstrated by the loss of both protein expression and its enzymatic activity on muscle tissue sample taken from the patient.
The patient presented, soon after birth, a progressive encephalopathy: hypotonia, progressive severe muscle atrophy, and respiratory failure. Serial brain Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) showed progressive abnormalities compatible with a metabolic disorder, possibly mitochondrial. Muscle biopsy disclosed moderate myopathic alterations and features consistent with a mitochondriopathy. The course was characterized by progressive worsening of the clinical and neurological picture, and the patient died at 5 months of age.
Regarding the in vitro analysis for mitochondrial aconitase activity, this study provides the first report on the validation on muscle from human subjects. To our knowledge, no prior reports have demonstrated correlation of phenotypic and diagnostic characteristics with in vitro muscle enzymatic activity of mitochondrial aconitase in humans.
In conclusion, this case further expands the genetic spectrum of ACO2 variants and defines a complex case of severe neonatal neurometabolic disorder.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA longitudinal analysis in a cohort of juvenile patients with late onset Pompe disease (LOPD)
PorcinoM MusumeciO ArenaIG ConsuloC UsbergoC RodolicoC ToscanoA 1Clinical and Experimental Medicine Department, University of Messina, Messina, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Late-Onset Pompe Disease (LOPD) includes patients from 1 year of age to adulthood. Among them, you can distinguish juvenile and adult patients. Heterogeneity in clinical manifestations and disease progression is not fully explained, but a better disease awareness allows an early diagnosis even in pre-symptomatic patients. However, short disease duration and young age seem predictors of good response to treatment.
To investigate disease progression and treatment response in juvenile patients, we retrospectively analyzed clinical, morphological, biochemical, and molecular aspects of 12 juvenile patients with LOPD. Longitudinal analysis of motor and respiratory functions assessed by 6MWT, GSGC, seated and supine FVC and muscle MRI findings was carried out.
Mean actual age is 16.6 years. All patients were diagnosed for isolated hyperCKemia and median age at diagnosis was 5 years. Median follow-up duration was 13.5 years. The commonest mutation was c.32-13T > G, found in compound heterozygosis in ten patients. Muscle biopsy showed vacuolar myopathy in 4/7 and unspecific changes in 3/7. Echocardiography was normal in all. Five patients developed proximal muscle weakness, characterized by mild waddling gait and positive Gowers manoeuver, from 6 months to 6 years after the diagnosis. Four of them started alglucosidase alfa and one avalglucosidase alfa. Patients on ERT showed motor and respiratory stability over time.
Juvenile patients represent an informative model for identifying prognostic factors and monitoring the disease progression under ERT. Early identification of clinical manifestations through a strict follow up is essential for a prompt treatment in young patients.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolThe ER-MITO (Emilia Romagna-Mitochondrial) project: prevalence and genetics of Chronic Progressive External Ophthalmoplegia (CPEO) in an Italian region
ValentinoML CaporaliL La MorgiaC PalomboF RomagnoliM FontiC MarescaA LiguoriR ZenesiniC D’AlessandroR CarelliVon behalf of the ER-MITO study group 1
IRCCS Institute of Neurological Sciences of Bologna, Bologna, Italy2 Department of Biomedical and NeuroMotor Sciences, University of Bologna, Bologna, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
The ER-MITO was a four-year multicenter program that connected the neurological units of the Emilia Romagna region with the aim to estimate the minimum prevalence of four most common mitochondrial phenotypes among which CPEO. A defined dataset was collected in a registry accessible to each participating unit.
86 adult patients with ptosis and/or ophthalmoplegia were recruited. A genetic defect was identified in 51 (62%) of which 26 (51%) carried a mtDNA mutation (17 a single deletion and 9 a mtDNA point mutation) and 25 (49%) a nDNA defect. TWNK was the most frequent nDNA gene (36%). The mtDNA patients presented with a lower age at first visit (52.7y) as compared to nDNA patients (63.5y) (p = 0.022), and with a lower age at onset of symptoms (26.1y) as compared to the nDNA group (44.6y) (p < 0.001). The onset before 18y was a distinguishing feature for the mtDNA-group (29% vs 5%) and none of them presented onset of symptoms after 60 y (p = 0.029). The distinctive clinical symptom was sensitive ataxia, which was more frequent in the nDNA-group (36%) as compared to the mtDNA group (7.7%) (p = 0.019). Finally, the presence of both RRF and COX-negative fibers distinguished the mtDNA-group (84%) from the nDNA-group (45%) (p = 0.015). The minimum prevalence of genetically defined CPEO was 1.35/100.000 inhabitants.
This study provides an estimate of minimum prevalence of CPEO in an Italian region, reveal mtDNA single deletion and TWNK gene as major genetic causes and highlight significant clinical differences between mtDNA and nDNA related CPEO.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolAccidental LOPD diagnosis in a young man with no clinical and instrumental evidence of neuromuscular dysfunction
SciaccoM LucchiariS GarbelliniM ComiG RonchiD 1 Neuromuscular and Rare Disease Unit, Fondazione IRCCS Ca’ Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Milan, Italy2 Dino Ferrari Center, Department of Pathophysiology and Transplantation, University of Milan, Milan, Italy3

## Abstract
We present the case of a 33 years old man who came to our observation because he was found affected with Late Onset Pompe Disease (LOPD) after he and his wife decided to undergo a genetic screening for autosomal recessive disorders when they learnt they were expecting a baby.
He is completely asymptomatic and all the tests he underwent are normal, namely neurological examination, serum CK levels, EMG, muscle MRI, respiratory and cardiac screening.
Biochemical analysis in lymphocytes disclosed the 93% reduction of of alpha-1,4-glucosidase residual activity in the proband and 30% of reduction in his parents, compared to controls.
GAA molecular analysis disclosed the “common” pathogenic substitution IVS1-32-13T > G in homozygosis in the proband. This splicing variant occurs in 90% of Caucasian late onset patients and is associated with a broad range of symptom onset. The variant was found heterozygous in DNA samples obtained in proband’s unaffected parents and in a chorionic villus sample collected during prenatal genetic testing. Several subjects displaying the homozygous IVS1 IVS1-32-13T > G have been described and many of them remain asymptomatic, although a subset develops symptoms with time. Our proband was negative for the synonymous variant c.510C > T which was demonstrated to act as a genetic modifier worsening the effect of the IVS1-32-13T > G mutation.
Given the absence of neuromuscular dysfunction, no replacing therapy has been so far started and the patient is being clinically monitored.
We are planning to repeat biochemical analysis (skin/muscle biopsy), to perform RNA studies as well as further molecular and protein investigations.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolExpanding the spectrum of clinical presentations associated with COA8 pathogenic variants
AntognozziS MagriF MeneriM GarbelliniM SalaniS FortunatoF RipoloneM ZanottiS CiscatoP SciaccoM ParenteV CortiS ComiG RonchiD 1
IRCCS Fondazione Ca’ Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Neurology Unit, Milan, Italy2 Dino Ferrari Center, Department of Pathophysiology and Transplantation, University of Milan, Milan, Italy3 IRCCS Fondazione Ca’ Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Neuromuscular and Rare Disease Unit, Milan, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Isolated mitochondrial respiratory chain Complex IV (Cytochrome c Oxidase or COX) deficiency is the second most frequent isolated respiratory chain defect. Causative mutations are mainly identified in structural COX subunits or in proteins involved in the maturation and assembly of the COX holocomplex.
We describe an Italian familial case of mitochondrial encephalomyopathy due to a variant in the COX assembly factor 8 gene (COA8).
Patient 1 is a 51-year-old woman who presented generalized epilepsy and retinitis pigmentosa at 10 years of age followed by hearing loss. Neurological examination shows bilateral ptosis, peripheral neuropathy, mild dysarthria and dysphonia, cognitive impairment, muscle weakness, cramps and myalgia after exercise. Marked brain atrophy was observed at MRI. Muscle biopsy had showed signs of mitochondrial dysfunction.
Patient 2, her sister, is a 50-year-old woman presenting fatigability, myalgia, and hearing loss. Neurological examination showed ptosis and muscle weakness. Muscle biopsy displayed a diffuse reduction of COX activity staining and some ragged-red fibers. Both sisters presented secondary amenorrhea.
After ruling out mtDNA mutations, WES analysis identified the novel homozygous COA8 defect (c170_173dupGACC, p.Pro59fs) in the probands.
Loss of function COA8 mutations have been associated with cavitating leukoencephalopathy with COX deficiency in 8 reported individuals. Disease course shows an early-onset rapid clinical deterioration affecting both cognitive and motor functions over months followed by stabilization and slow improvement over several years.
Our findings expand the clinical spectrum of COA8-related disease. We confirm the benign course of this rare disorder, highlighting its (intrafamilial) clinical variability.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA primary cardiological phenotype caused by an inherited mtDNA single deletion: a case report from an Italian pedigree
LoprioreP NeuhoferC MontanoV MeliA Lo GerfoA CaligoMA BeruttiR KopajtichR SicilianoG ProkischH MancusoM 1 Department of Clinical and Experimental Medicine, Neurological Institute, University of Pisa, Pisa, Italy2

## Abstract
Progressive external ophthalmoplegia (PEO), Kearns-Sayre syndrome (KSS) and Pearson syndrome are the three classical overlapping phenotypes associated with single large mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) deletions, which are the first mtDNA aberration to be associated with human mitochondrial disorders. mtDNA deletion syndromes usually occur as simplex cases (i.e., a single occurrence in a family), as single large mtDNA deletions mostly arise de novo. For offspring of affected women harboring a single large mtDNA deletion, the recurrence risk is considered to be low and has been estimated at 1:24. Ptosis and/or ophthalmoplegia are hallmark symptoms of PEO and represent the common basis of the syndromic continuum related to mtDNA single large deletions. Within this continuum, cardiomyopathy is a phenotypic feature which defines “KSS spectrum” but has never been reported to occur alone or as a cardinal aspect.
Here we report the cases of an Italian 71-yrs old woman and her two daughters (of 43 and 49 yrs of age) suffering of early-adulthood onset diabetes mellitus and bilateral sensorineural hearing loss (MIDD). The two daughters developed a severe dilated cardiomyopathy and an atrio-ventricular block during the third decade of life (one of them received an hearth transplantation). The mother developed a bilateral ptosis, exercise intolerance and dilated cardiomyopathy in her fifties, and parkinsonism at the age of 66-yrs. Brain CT revealed basal ganglia calcification and cerebral atrophy. Muscle biopsy showed unspecific myopathic changes and normal electron transport chain activity. To date, none of the daughters have developed PEO or other myopathic features as well as any other mitochondrial manifestations. Molecular analysis revealed a large heteroplasmic mtDNA single deletion in both urine and blood in the three patients. Heteroplasmy quantification and deletion breakpoints sequencing are ongoing. WES in trio did not reveal further pathogenic variants.
Our case demonstrates a primary cardiological mitochondrial phenotype, associated with MIDD, caused by an inherited heteroplasmic mtDNA single deletion. This unusual eventuality, as well as the absence of PEO, has complicated the path to reach a molecular diagnosis. We suggest mitochondrial deletions should be considered as possible disease cause also in unusual mitochondrial phenotypes and familial cases.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolSudoscan in Pompe disease: a preliminary study
LupicaA AlongeP TorrenteA Di StefanoV BrighinaF CrescimannoG 1 Department of Biomedicine, Neuroscience and advanced Diagnostic (BIND), University of Palermo, Italy2 Italian National Research Council, Institute for Biomedical Research and Innovation, Palermo, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Acid maltase deficiency (Pompe disease) is an autosomal recessive glycogenosis with multisystemic involvement. A neuropathic and neurovegetative impairment has been reported.
However, because data on small-fibers are lacking we propose SUDOSCAN as a reliable instrument to evaluate small-fibers involvement in Pompe disease.
6 patients referring to our Clinic were evaluated using SFNSCL questionnaire (Small FIbers Neuropathy Screening LIst).
Electrochemical skin conductance (ESC) of hands and feet was assessed using SUDOSCAN. Two tests were run to increase reliability. Results were compared to controls matched by sex and age.
Patients were also examined for motor and respiratory impairment by manual muscle testing, WGM, 6MWT and routinely pneumological assessment. Correlation analysis between questionnaire results, clinical measures and SUDOSCAN values were performed. A significative correlation was observed between reduced ESC, disease duration(r = -0.923, p = 0.009) and acid maltase residual activity (r = -0.957, p = 0.003).
Our study, even with the limit of few patients, supports the hypothesis of a small fibers involvement in Pompe Disease. This datum, obtained with a simple and fast tool, requires futher investigatons as it can contribute to reveal unexplored disabling aspects in Pompe disease and can play a role as an outcome measure in this complex disorder.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolHigh dose intravenous corticosteroids in refractory Immune Checkpoint Inhibitor-related Myocarditis/Myositis/Myasthenia overlap syndrome
TufanoL LaulettaA MorinoS MusumeciB AntoniniG GaribaldiM 1NESMOS Department, Sapienza University of Rome, Italy1 Cardiology, Clinical and Molecular Medicine Department, Sapienza University of Rome, Sant’Andrea Hospital, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Immune Checkpoint Inhibitors (ICIs) represent a major progress in cancer therapy. However, their mechanism of action may result in a spectrum of immune-related adverse events, among which neuromuscular and cardiac complications.
Herein we report on a case of a 47-year-old woman with metastatic lung adenocarcinoma who received first line therapy with Nivolumab and Ipilimumab associated with Carboplatin and Pemetrexed. Two weeks after the first infusion she developed fluctuating ptosis, ophthalmoplegia with diplopia, dysphagia and dyspnea, associated with mild distal weakness of upper limbs. ECG, laboratory exams and echocardiography showed data suggestive of myocarditis with troponin over 13.000 pg/ml. CK levels were also elevated over 6000 UI/L. Extensor digitorum
communis muscle biopsy revealed inflammatory findings consistent with myositis. Repetitive nerve stimulation, anti-AChR and anti-MuSK antibodies resulted negative. She started prednisone 1 mg/kg/day, IVIg (2 gr/kg) and pyridostigmine 60 mg tid with only mild reduction of CK levels. Because of persistently elevated troponin and poor clinical response, she received intravenous methylprednisolone 1 gr/day for 5 days followed by striking reduction of troponin levels and clinical improvement. At one-month follow-up, no laboratory signs of myocarditis and myositis were detectable with regression of distal upper limb weakness and milder ophthalmoparesis and ptosis. Myocarditis/Myositis/Myasthenia overlap syndrome is an increasingly reported life-threatening adverse event related to ICIs which must be promptly diagnosed and treated.
Intravenous methylprednisolone could represent an effective first line therapy in case of severe ICIs-related Myocarditis/Myositis/Myasthenia association.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolCognitive function assessment in a cohort of patients with Myotonic Dystrophy type 1: a single-center experience
RisiB Cotti PiccinelliS FerrariE CariaF DamioliS BertellaE LabellaB PoliL BonitoV PadovaniA FilostoM 1
NeMO-Brescia Clinical Center for Neuromuscular Diseases, Brescia, Italy2 Department of Clinical and Experimental Sciences, University of Brescia, Italy3 Unit of Neurology, ASST “Spedali Civili”, Brescia, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
DM1-related cognitive dysfunctions have been studied for a long time and an early and accelerated ageing process has been suggested. Although the worsening in cognitive scores seems to correlate with age and disease duration but not with CTG repeats, muscular impairment and education at baseline, the evolution of the cognitive profile and whether decline could be global or process-specific are still a matter of debate.
The aim of this study was to further characterize the relation between cognitive and clinical features in an Italian DM1 cohort.
We enrolled consecutive DM1 patients who underwent a neuropsychological battery investigating five domains: memory (Digit Span, Rey’s 15 Word List, Story test and Rey’s complex figure); attention (TMT, Stroop test, Symbol Digit); logic-operational skills (FAB, Raven Matrices, Cognitive Estimation task); language (Word Fluency); visuo-constructive skills (Copy of Rey’s complex figure, Clock drawing). Single-domain impairment was defined by the presence of at least one pathological subtest. Demographic and clinical variables were collected for the appropriate correlation studies.
Twenty patients (male, n = 13; age at onset≥40, n = 6) were evaluated at baseline and twelve of them also at follow-up (n = 3 < 12 months, n = 7 at 12 months, n = 2 at 18 months). At baseline, n = 6 had impairment in one domain, n = 3 in two domains, n = 1 in three, four and five domains respectively. The most frequently compromised domain was that of visuo-constructive skills (in n = 8), followed by logic-operational skills and memory (both in n = 6). The number of involved domains correlated with 6MWT (r = -0.709, p = 0.015) in an adjusted analysis and was greater in late-onset patients (p = 0.015). Worsening at follow-up, represented by an increase in the number of affected domains, occurred in 4 out of 12 patients; no clinical variable at baseline was found to be associated with this worsening.
Our study confirms the involvement of multiple cognitive domains in DM1 with predilection for the visuo-constructive and executive domains. Cognitive load is independent from several motor tasks and seems to correlate with walking impairment, reinforcing the theory of a cognitive-motor interaction underlying gait.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolClinical and molecular characterization of two siblings affected by Brody Myopathy
VelardoD AntognozziS RimoldiM PagliaraniS CogiamanianF BarbieriS CortiS ComiG RonchiD 1

## Abstract
Brody Myopathy is a muscle disorder characterized by childhood onset exercise-induced progressive impairment of muscle relaxation, stiffness, cramps, and myalgia, predominantly in four limbs and eyelids. Symptoms generally improve after a few minutes of rest and may be exacerbated by cold. This disorder is recessively inherited and associated with pathogenic variants in the ATP2A1 gene encoding for the Sarco- and Endoplasmic Reticulum Calcium ATPase protein SERCA1.
SERCA1 catalyzes the ATP-dependent uptake of Ca2+ from the cytosol to sarcoplasmic reticulum taking part in the regulation of calcium levels and therefore muscle contraction. In Brody myopathy patients, the activity of SERCA1 in type II muscle fibres is reduced, resulting in delayed muscle relaxation, silent cramps, muscle weakness and muscle atrophy. Since SERCA1 is uniquely expressed in type II skeletal muscle fibres, muscle stiffness in Brody disease is primarily triggered by rapid, intense contractions.
Although about 40 patients from 28 different families have been reported so far, our knowledge about disease progression and pathogenesis are far from being exhaustive.
Here we report the clinical, instrumental, and molecular features of two siblings presenting childhood-onset exercise-induced muscle stiffness without pain. Both the probands display difficulty in climbing stairs and running, frequent falls, delayed muscle relaxation after exertion. Cold temperatures worsen these symptoms. No myotonic discharges were observed at electromyography. Whole Exome Sequencing analysis revealed the presence of two ATP2A1 variants: the previously reported frameshift microdeletion c.2464delC and the likely pathogenic novel splice-site variant c.324+1G > A, whose detrimental effect was demonstrated in ATP2A1 transcript analysis. The bi-allelic inheritance was verified by Sanger sequencing in the unaffected parents. This study expands the molecular defects associated with Brody myopathy.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolMyotonic Dystrophy type 1: motor and cardio-respiratory evolution trajectories
DiellaE LoMauroA Delle FaveM CanellaG MeolaG CimaR D’AngeloMG 1
IRCCS Eugenio Medea, Bosisio Parini, Italy2 Dipartimento di Elettronica, Informazione e Bioingegneria, Politecnico di Milano, Milan, Italy3
Università degli Studi di Milano, Milan, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Myotonic dystrophy type 1 (DM1) is the most prevalent muscular dystrophy. It is a multisystemic disorder with dysfunction of many organs and tissues and a great phenotypical variability. Motor involvement is often associated with cardio-pulmonary and cognitive impairments. Up to now, few studies collected longitudinal natural history data of DM1 in order to draw evolution trajectories useful either to improve clinical management either to reach trial readiness.
Out of the 92 DM1 patients followed in our center along 20 years of follow up, we extracted the data of patients with more than three periodic motor and cardio-respiratory function evaluations. We considered 42 patients: 5 with congenital form of DM1, 10 with childhood form and 27 with adult form.
Motor involvement was evaluated with selective muscle strength (through Medical Research Council grading), motor function tasks (through Motor Function Measure) and walking endurance (through 6-minute walk test). Cardio-pulmonary function through spirometry tests (Forced Vital Capacity, Total Lung Capacity), nocturnal oxygen saturation, cardiac ultrasound (LVEF) and 24 hours electrocardiogram.
Our exploratory observation (through the collection of more than 400 visits with more than 60 parameters collected at each visit) seems to show that motor and cardio-respiratory deterioration starts around adolescence in congenital forms and above twenty years of age in childhood forms. The slight decline of these functions in adult forms seems combined with the loss of gait endurance around the mean age of fifty years.
The definition of disease evolution trajectories (motor, cardiac and respiratory) is essential to estimate the efficacy of new therapeutic approaches (i.e. gene modulating therapies) in myotonic dystrophies.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA novel hypokalemic periodic paralysis mutation in the SCN4A gene shared by two families and description of a homozygous patient
PagliaraniS SansoneV GerardiF ScarlatoM PrevitaliSC LucchiariS MeolaG ComiG P 1
Foundation IRCCS Ca’ Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Neurology Unit, Milan, Italy2 The NeMO Clinical Center – Neurorehabilitation Unit, University of Milan, Milan, Italy3
Istituto Clinico Quarenghi, San Pellegrino Terme, Bergamo, Italy4
Institute of Experimental Neurology (InSpe) and Division of Neuroscience, IRCCS Ospedale San Raffaele, Milan, Italy5 Neuroscience Section, Dino Ferrari Centre, Department of Pathophysiology and Transplantation (DEPT), University of Milan, Milan, Italy6 Department of Neurorehabilitation Sciences, Casa Di Cura Igea, Milan, Italy; Fondazione Malattie Miotoniche, Milan, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Hypokaliemic periodic paralysis (HypoPP) is a rare genetic disease characterized by episodes of flaccid paralysis associated with low serum potassium. However, many patients describe more modest episodes of generalized weakness that do not result in overt paralysis. Triggers for episodes of weakness could be exercise, food intake, cold temperature. HypoPP is dominantly inherited and is caused by mutations in CACNA1S or SCN4A genes. In most cases mutations are found in S4 transmembrane segments and involve the arginines which function as voltage sensors.
We report two unrelated families harbouring a novel mutation in SCN4A causing HypoPP. Patients from both families experienced episodes of weakness associated with low serum potassium levels. One patient from family 1 showed elevated CK and the two patients of family 2 showed lid-lag (mother and son). Both families shared the same c.791T > C, p.Phe264Ser mutation. Phe264Ser is located in the fifth segment of the first domain of Nav1.4. This variant is not described in GnomAD nor in Clinvar and bioinformatic analysis predicted this variant as pathogenic. ACMG rules classified c.791T > C, p.Phe264Ser as likely pathogenic. In each family, c.791T > C segregated with the disease, but we found that in family 2 the mother was homozygous for the variant. She had a very early onset at the age of 3 years and she experienced 4/5 episodes of weakness per year before starting acetazolamide therapy.
We have described a novel mutation causing HypoPP and one of the few HypoPP patients with a homozygous mutation expanding the knowledge about this disease.

## Abstract
Genetic myopathies represent a heterogeneous group of muscle disorders, which can exhibit overlapping clinical features with other neuromuscular conditions, including idiopathic inflammatory myopathies (IIM). This can lead to difficulties in diagnosing and treating patients appropriately.
This study aimed to retrospectively screen patients registered in the IIM database at San Raffaele Hospital since 2016 and identify characteristics of patients that presented a final diagnosis of genetic myopathy. We assessed the clinical, laboratory, histologic, and instrumental findings, as well as the genetic testing results of the identified patients.
Of 72 patients, initially diagnosed with myositis and treated accordingly, four (5.6%) that underwent further investigation due to ambiguous clinical, biochemical, and/or instrumental presentations, were finally diagnosed with genetic myopathy. All patients had creatine phosphokinase elevation, clinical/anamnestic features that were suggestive of IIM, and initial response to a corticosteroid course. One patient also showed positivity to myositis-specific antibodies (anti-Mi-2). All but one case fulfilled EULAR/ACR diagnostic criteria for a probable diagnosis of IIM. Despite appropriate immunosuppressive therapy, patients showed persistent clinical unresponsiveness, prompting further investigation with a repetition of muscle biopsy and next-generation sequencing (NGS), which revealed a revised diagnosis of genetic myopathy (ACTN2, ANO5, NEB, and RYR1).
We suggest that clinicians should be aware of the possibility of misdiagnosis and consider genetic testing in cases of IIM unresponsive to immunosuppressive therapy. Integration of clinical, biochemical, and instrumental data such as muscle MRI and EMG may aid in differentiating between the two conditions, leading to a more accurate diagnosis and appropriate treatment.

AburahmaSK ShboulM RousanLA LucchiariS BiellaF ComiGP MeolaG PagliaraniS 1 Department of Pediatrics, Faculty of Medicine, Jordan University of Science and Technology, Irbid, Jordan2 Department of Laboratory Sciences, Faculty of Medicine, Jordan University of Science and Technology, Irbid, Jordan3 Department of Radiology, Faculty of Medicine, Jordan University of Science and Technology, Irbid, Jordan4 Neuroscience Section, Dino Ferrari Centre, Department of Pathophysiology and Transplantation(EPT), University of Milan, Milan, Italy5

## Abstract
Skeletal muscle contraction is mediated by excitation-contraction coupling (ECC), a process involving the rapid elevation of cytosolic calcium. The dihydropyridine receptor (DHPR) is located on T-tubule membranes and is the voltage sensors of ECC. DHPR is encoded by the CACNA1S gene which has been recently reported as cause of a severe congenital myopathy.
We describe 2 sisters with compound heterozygous mutations involving the CACNA1S gene. Both had neonatal hypotonia, muscle weakness, and delayed motor milestones. Prior to one year of age, periodic paralysis was evident as episodic flaccidity and loss of postural control. Periodic paralysis episodes continued to occur most notably with cold exposure, lasting around 10 minutes each.
Lab evaluation revealed normal CPK. Electrophysiological evaluation revealed normal motor and sensory nerve conduction studies, and normal long exercise testing. Limb cooling and cold provocation led to clinical weakness and a drop in motor amplitude.
Magnetic resonance imaging of the older sister showed symmetrical tigroid pattern fat replacement and atrophy of bilateral gluteus maximus muscles.
NGS revealed two variants in the CACNA1S gene: both c.3526-2A > G and c.2831G > A (p.Cys944Tyr) are not described in the gnomAD database. Both variants co-segregated into affected sisters. The c.3526-2A > G is expected to disrupt the acceptor splice site of intron 27 by in silico analysis, whereas the c.2831G > A variant replaces a highly conserved cysteine residue with tyrosine at codon 944 in the DIII-S5 of DHPR.
This novel phenotype of a milder congenital recessive myopathy and early onset periodic paralysis expands the phenotypic spectrum of CACNA1S gene mutations.

## Abstract
Inflammatory responses to immune stimuli can be associated with different types of muscular involvement and may be difficult to detect in time, particularly when they are masked by an insidious onset of symptoms.
A 37-year-old woman comes to our attention for generalized asthenia and myalgias starting at the hands and then diffused, also reporting an isolated transitory diplopia episode, these symptoms begun few days after a first dosage of Astrazeneca vaccine she received several months before. Her family history included the mother with Sjogren Syndrome and two maternal cousins with Systemic Sclerosis. Her blood exams showed a peak of antinuclear antibodies (ANA) which later on normalized, and hyperCKemia (650-800 U/L), while inflammation markers were normal. Of note, both electromyography and myositis associated antibodies were normal as well. Neurologic examination during a first visit showed convergent strabismus in the left eye, diplopia, but no sign of fatigable weakness. The symptoms notwithstanding started to progress over some weeks and evolved in a severe weakness in the shoulder girdle with inability to completely abduct the upper limbs. Muscle MRI of the lower limbs showed moderate oedema of the legs and a more severe oedema in the thighs. We decided to perform a muscle biopsy that revealed inflammatory cellular infiltrations consistent with the diagnosis of myositis. Meanwhile, Ach-R antibodies tested positive and we decided to start a high dose steroids therapy with partial improvements of symptoms. Currently, the patient is still taking steroids awaiting to start intravenous immunoglobulin.

## Abstract
Schwartz–Jampel Syndrome type 1 (SJS1), also known as chondrodystrophic myotonia, is a rare autosomal recessive musculoskeletal disorder characterized by permanent myotonic myopathy and skeletal dysplasia, caused by various mutations in the HSPG2 gene encoding protein perlecan, a major component of basement membranes.
We report a Moroccan child with clinical features of SJS1. He presented blepharophimosis, micrognathia, low-set ears and coxa valga. He showed progressive stiffness and myalgia leading to difficult autonomous gait and masticatory functions. Myotonia was not observed but difficulty in muscle release after maximum contraction of the quadriceps was disclosed. EMG showed myopathic abnormalities associated with repetitive activity almost subcontinua, also with myotonic aspects. Muscle biopsy revealed unspecific muscle suffering with specific aspects such as “core”. Two compound heterozygous mutations in the HSPG2 gene were detected using next-generation sequencing: a novel splice mutation c.3888+1G > A and a described mutation c.8464G > A, loading to exon 31 and 64 skipping in mRNA, respectively. Both parents harbored one heterozygous mutation of the proband and did not exhibit any skeletal abnormalities. Real-time and immunostaining analysis revealed a down regulation of the HSPG2 gene and a weaker staining in the muscle of the patient than in the control specimen. The results of the present study suggest that the two compound heterozygous mutations identified in the HSPG2 gene may be responsible for inducing the SJS1 phenotype our patient.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA case of Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis initially misdiagnosed for myositis
MeoniC BianchiF BecattiniL FontanelliL GiovanniniB SicilianoG 1University of Pisa, Department of Clinical and Experimental Medicine, Neurological Clinic, Pisa, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS) is a neurodegenerative disorder involving motor neurons. Symptoms of muscular hyper-excitability like cramps and fasciculation related to lower motor neurons impairment may remain isolated for a while, and in this phase, the electromyogram may only show isolated fasciculation potentials. Fasciculations and cramps are common also in myopathies, in myositis, or in other autoimmune disorders. All these factors may contribute in making the diagnosis challenging, mainly when these diseases overlap.
Here we describe the case of a man suffering from muscular cramps at first suspected for myositis, who later developed ALS. A 56-year-old man was in charge at Rheumatology Department for a history of isolated muscular pain and cramps started two years prior, and progressively worsened. At that time, the electromyogram only showed isolated fasciculation potentials. The blood tests showed normal creatine-kinase levels but slightly positive myositis-related antibodies, and a capillaroscopy showed an uncertain microangiopathy. However, rheumatologists ruled-out a myositis. Afterwards, the patient developed left limbs hyposthenia. The neurological examination revealed diffuse fasciculations, hyposthenia in abductor pollicis and in common digital extensor of left hand, and in left tibialis anterior muscle. He repeated an electromyogram showing neurogenic pattern in left tibialis anterior and in left interosseous, and fasciculation potentials in all tested muscles.
ALS and myopathies may share similar symptoms. Sometimes these diseases may overlap, whereas sometimes symptoms of muscular hyper-excitability may be a sort of prodromal ALS. An accurate biological, clinical and neurophysiological follow-up is mandatory to differentiate these peculiar clinical pictures.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolLong-term treatment and follow up in adult patients with anti-HMGRC immune-mediated myopathy
VercelliL GadaletaG UrbanoG BrodiniG Chiadò-PiatL RumboloF ManettaT MonginiT 1 Neuromuscular Unit, Department of Neurosciences “Rita Levi Montalcini”, Turin, Italy2 S.C. Biochimica Clinica, AOU Città della Salute e della Scienza, Turin, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Immune-mediated myopathies (IMM) are acquired myopathies often characterized by the presence of specific autoantibodies, with variable muscular involvement. Antibodies against 3-hydroxy-3-methylglutaryl-coenzyme A reductase (HMGCR) recognize a group of necrotizing myopathies with a progressive proximal muscle weakness and highly elevated creatine kinase (CK) levels, often associated with statins exposure.
In our Center we identified 12 patients (5 female) with anti-HMGCR related myopathy (age range at onset 17-79 years). Serum CK ranged from 1000 IU/L to 28000 IU/L; 83% presented a progressive proximal muscle weakness especially in pelvic girdle. Three patients were paucisymptomatic with severe hyperCKemia.
Ten/12 patients had a statin exposure. Cardio-pulmonary involvement was excluded; 25% had dysphagia; two males had a neoplastic history. Muscle biopsies (7 cases) revealed a necrotic pattern with poor inflammatory signs. In 4 cases we discovered anti-HMGCR positive status some years after their disease onset. All patients were treated with oral steroids with partial clinical improvements and reduction in CK values; all patients started intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIg) treatment with good improvement and normal daily life activities. Three cases were treated also with an immunosuppressive agent (azathioprine).
When IVIg therapy was withdrawn in five patients, rapid clinical worsening and/or increase of CK values appeared, with improvement upon resumption of IVIg treatment, and stabilization in clinical follow-up.
Anti HMGCR-myopathy is a relatively new form of IIM; the gold standard treatment is not established and often relays on clinical experience. We observed a long term, good response to IVIg in all patients, with a chronic dependance to immunomodulant therapy.

## Abstract
Myotonic dystrophy type 1 (DM1) is often diagnosed during pregnancy in women or after the development of symptoms in one parent. This reflects in missed prenatal diagnosis in children affected. However, the impact of the parental diagnostic delay on the prognosis of their children is not established.
This study aims to describe the perinatal characteristics and the developmental outcomes of patients with congenital (CDM) and childhood (ChDM) forms in relation to the timing of diagnosis of the affected parent.
Retrospective study of children with CDM or ChDM followed by 12 Italian centres. Children’s disease type and parental timing of diagnosis was recorded. CTG expansions were classified as E1 = < 500,E2 = 500–1.000,E3 = 1.000–1.500,E4 > 1.500.Perinatal features, motor and cognitive development were collected.
Seventy-one children were included, 41CDM, mean (± SD) age 12.0 ± 5.1y, 30ChDM mean age 12.7 ± 4.8y. All CDM had maternal inheritance. Only 6/41 were tested antenatally given the known maternal diagnosis; 2 children born to mothers affected were only diagnosed at birth.
Perinatal information was available for 21 children;4 were born preterm, 15/21 required NICU > 48 hours, 16/19 had ambulation and 14/18 speech delay. 2/21 had normal QI.
Of the 30 ChDM, 17 had paternal inheritance. 6/30 were diagnosed in-utero given the known parental diagnosis. Information was available in 19 children. All were born at term, one required NICU. 1/19 had ambulation and 4/19 speech delay. 7/13 had normal QI.
Diagnostic delay in DM1 represent still a significant issue especially in females of child-bearing age. It has a significant impact on the pre-and perinatal management of children affected.

## Abstract
Myopathology is a known phenomenon in the oncology setting such as cancer-myositis association, with tumor release of proinflammatory cytokines increasing systemic and activating muscle inflammation and degeneration. In addition, cancer management (local and/or systemic therapies), potentially impact nontargeted tissues as muscle. Most studies focus cancer–host interaction, overlooking the capability of radio/chemotherapy to induce myopathy itself.
We described two patients that developed a non-inflammatory myopathy as a side-effect of different anticancer medications.
Patient 1 is a 71-year-old woman with metastatic non-small cell lung cancer, treated with Osimertinib, an epidermal growth factor receptor tyrosine kinase inhibitor. After treatment initiation, she developed a slowly progressive axial muscle weakness, predominantly involving paravertebral muscles, which made her unable to sustain the trunk and walk unassisted. EMG showed a myopathic pattern without pathologic spontaneous activity. Patient 2 is a 11-years-old boy with metastatic medulloblastoma of the posterior cranial fossa treated with neurosurgery, chemotherapy according to INT protocol (methotrexate, vincristine, etoposide, cyclophosphamide, carboplatin), radiotherapy on the craniospinal axis and autologous transplant. After treatment, he developed proximal muscle weakness, which made him unable to walk. MRI showed a marked alteration in signal intensity of almost all the muscles of the pelvis. Both patients had mildly elevated or normal serum CK and negative myositis-specific/-associated antibodies. Muscle biopsies of both patients revealed marked fiber atrophy, with slight signs of type-grouping, and different level of mitochondrial damage without signs of inflammation, suggesting that muscle damage of different nature from myositis should be considered among the side effects of radio/chemotherapy.

## Abstract
Inclusion body myositis (IBM) is an acquired muscle disease presenting in late adult age and clinically characterized by involvement of finger flexor and quadriceps muscles. Inclusion body myositis–functional rating scale (IBM-FRS) is a validated scale investigating different aspects of the disease, in particular daily activities. However, longitudinal data on IBM-FRS or analysis of single items of this scale are poor in literature. This would improve our understanding of IBM natural history and even patients stratifications and clinical trial design.
Our aim is to investigate correlations between IBM-FRS and demographic and clinical variables at baseline and after 1-year follow-up. We showed here preliminary data at baseline.
In this monocentric study, we revised computerized charts of patients with a previous diagnosis of IBM, according to the European NeuroMuscular Centre (ENMC) criteria. Correlations between variables were investigated with Mann-Whitney test or Spearman’s correlation coefficient with the corresponding p-value, as appropriate.
We included 34 patients (12 females, 35%). Ten patients (29%) had a clinico-pathologically defined diagnosis, 14 (41%) clinically defined, and 10 (29%) probable according to the ENMC criteria.
Median age at evaluation was 74 years (range 51 – 87), median disease duration was 110 months (range 15-256). Median IBM-FRS-TS was 21 (range 3-34). No significant correlation was found between IBM-FRS-TS and sex (p = 0.35), age at diagnosis (p = 0.71) or disease duration (p = 0.08).
Total IBM-FRS score did not correlate to demographic and clinical variables in our cohort at baseline. Subanalysis, including correlations with single IBM items, is ongoing.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolPreclinical Data Support the Initiation of the ACHIEVE Trial of DYNE-101 in Individuals with Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1)
DelageE CotaliE WolfD PicarielloT SchlaefkeL RussoR ChangA HildebrandS NajimJ QiuQ WeedenT DavisJWII MixC HanB ZanottiS BeskrovnayaO FarwellW 1Dyne Therapeutics Inc., Waltham, MA, USA162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Myotonic dystrophy type 1 (DM1) is caused by expansion of CUG repeats in the 3’-untranslated region of the dystrophia myotonica protein kinase (DMPK) RNA. The expanded CUG repeats form hairpin-loop structures that sequester splicing regulators into toxic nuclear foci, leading to a spliceopathy that drives DM1 clinical manifestations. No disease-modifying treatments are approved, limiting current treatment to symptom management. We developed the FORCE™ platform, which harnesses the natural expression of transferrin receptor (TfR)1 on muscle cells for targeted delivery of oligonucleotides. DYNE-101 is a TfR1-targeting antigen-binding fragment conjugated to a gapmer antisense oligonucleotide that targets nuclear DMPK RNA. In hTfR1/DMSXL mice, a novel model of DM1, DYNE-101 led to reduction of toxic human DMPK RNA and a corresponding splicing correction in cardiac and skeletal muscle that were accompanied by reduction of DMPK nuclear foci in the heart. Additionally, DYNE-101 led to a substantial suppression of WT DMPK expression in cardiac, skeletal, and smooth muscle after repeat monthly dosing in non-human primates, and had a favorable safety profile. These data supported the initiation of the ACHIEVE trial, a randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled, multiple ascending dose (MAD) Phase 1/2 study assessing safety, tolerability, pharmacodynamics, and pharmacokinetics of DYNE-101 administered intravenously to ~64 adults with DM1 aged 18-49 years (NCT05481879). The primary outcome is the number of participants with treatment-emergent adverse events. Change from baseline in splicing index in skeletal muscle is a secondary outcome. The ACHIEVE study will inform the potential of DYNE-101 as a treatment for DM1.

## Abstract
Vamorolone is a dissociative steroidal anti-inflammatory drug that seeks to retain efficacy and reduce safety concerns in patients with Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy compared to corticosteroids via changes to structure/activity relationships with the glucocorticoid receptor.
The objective of this analysis is to evaluate the efficacy and safety in patients who switched from prednisone to vamorolone in the second period of the study VISION-DMD (VBP15-004) is a 48-week randomized, double-blind study consisting of two periods. During Period 1, 121 patients were randomized 1:1:1:1 to vamorolone 2 or 6 mg/kg/day, prednisone 0.75 mg/kg/day or placebo for 24 weeks. During Period 2, patients initiated on vamorolone continued their assigned treatment and those on placebo or prednisone crossed over to treatment with vamorolone 2 or 6 mg/kg/day. Changes in efficacy outcomes were assessed by time to stand velocity (TTSTANDV), 6-minute walk distance (6MWT), 10-meter run/walk velocity (TTRWV), NSAA score and 4-stair climb velocity (TTCLIMBV).
Out of 121 patients, 30 received prednisone during Period 1 followed by vamorolone treatment during Period 2. All 30 completed vamorolone treatment to Week 48. Efficacy was maintained across all functional endpoints after switching from prednisone to vamorolone 6 mg/kg/day. No significant differences were seen at Week 48 in any efficacy endpoints in the switch population versus patients continuously treated with vamorolone 6 mg/kg/day throughout the study. No serious adverse events were reported after the switch. Annualized rates of adverse events were reduced after the switch from prednisone to vamorolone (all events: 20% reduction, steroid-related events: 40% reduction). Stunting of growth observed with prednisone during Period 1 was reversed during treatment with vamorolone during Period 2. There was no loss of efficacy after switching from prednisone to vamorolone 6 mg/kg/day. After the switch, the rate of adverse events on vamorolone was reduced compared to that reported on prednisone and stunting of growth reversed.

## Abstract
Vamorolone is a dissociative steroidal anti-inflammatory drug that seeks to retain efficacy and reduce safety concerns in patients with DMD compared to corticosteroids via changes to structure/activity relationships with the glucocorticoid receptor. The objective of this analysis is to evaluate efficacy and safety of continuous 48-week vamorolone treatment.
VISION-DMD (VBP15-004) is a 48-week randomized, double-blind study consisting of two periods. During Period 1, 121 patients were randomized 1:1:1:1 to vamorolone 2 or 6 mg/kg/day, prednisone 0.75 mg/kg/day or placebo for 24 weeks. During Period 2, patients continued their initial vamorolone dose or crossed over to vamorolone 2 or 6 mg/kg/day. Changes from baseline to Week 48 in efficacy outcomes; time to stand velocity (TTSTANDV), 6-minute walk distance (6MWT), 10-meter run/walk velocity (TTRWV), NSAA score and 4-stair climb velocity (TTCLIMBV) were analyzed with MMRM.
Of the 121 patients, 56 received vamorolone during both Period 1 and 2. Two of 56 patients discontinued treatment during Period 2 (1 adverse event, 1 consent withdrawn). For vamorolone 6 mg/kg/day, efficacy was maintained or further improved from Week 24 to Week 48, whilst for vamorolone 2 mg/kg/day, efficacy was maintained in some efficacy endpoints but not all. Differences between dose levels were seen in TTSTAND (p = 0.001), 6MWT (p = 0.047) and TTCLIMB (p = 0.031), while not in TTRW (p = 0.375) or NSAA (p = 0.602). Three serious adverse events were reported during the 48 weeks: perforated appendicitis (2 mg/kg/day), asthma (6 mg/kg/day) and viral gastroenteritis (6 mg/kg/day), all considered unrelated to vamorolone. Stunting of growth was not seen and body mass index stabilized after an initial increase during the first 24 weeks.
Efficacy of vamorolone 6 mg/kg was maintained throughout 48 weeks across all outcome measures, whilst only across some measures for 2mg/kg/day. Vamorolone treatment was generally well tolerated on both dose levels throughout 48 weeks.

## Abstract
We describe a family with recurrence of Duchenne muscular dystrophy in six males belonging to two generations; the affected are all deceased and the disease causing mutation is unknown.
In two obliged carriers, mothers and daughters of affected, MLPA analysis excluded deletions/duplications in the DMD gene and NGS analysis was negative for point mutation as well.
Linkage analysis with polymorphic markers spanning the dystrophin gene locus was carried on in several family member including the two obliged carriers and the two healthy grandchildren of one of them.
The analysis allowed the identification of the X chromosome shared by the obliged carriers and not by their healthy grandchildren, therefore presumed to harbour the mutation causing the disease. The daughter of one of the carriers showed an anomalous segregation of two markers (DXS1067 and DXS1036) and she was apparently missing the maternal allele.
MLPA analysis of DMD gene in this female revealed a heterozygous non contiguous deletion of exons 51-52 and 63-66. We speculate that this genomic rearrangement is the clue of a more complex genomic configuration in the DMD gene in this family at the basis of recurrence of Duchenne muscular dystrophy and we will explore this hypothesis performing whole genome analysis in the female with the non contiguous deletion and in her mother, obliged carrier.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolErgoreflex Sensitivity is Associated with Cardiac Involvement in Becker Muscular Dystrophy
CastiglioneV SciarroneP TorriF BorrelliC BarisonA- TodiereG GrigoratosC SicilianoG PassinoC EmdinM RicciG GiannoniA 1 “Health Science” Interdisciplinary Research Center, Scuola Superiore Sant’Anna, Pisa, Italy2

## Abstract
To assess the association between ergoreflex sensitivity, a marker of skeletal muscle work regulating ventilatory and autonomic responses to exercise, and cardio-muscular involvement in Becker muscular dystrophy (BMD), fifteen male BMD patients (median age 37 years [25-42]) underwent a comprehensive evaluation including ECG, biohumoral assessment, cardiac magnetic resonance (CMR), and ergoreflex determination with a handgrip exercise test. We distinguished muscular phenotype into “classic” (proximal weakness affecting the shoulders and pelvis with waddling gait) or “mild” and defined cardiac involvement based on the presence of late gadolinium enhancement (LGE) at CMR.
Ten patients (67%) had a “classic” muscular phenotype. Nine patients (60%) had LGE at CMR (median number of segments 9 [6-12]). The percentage of patients with cardiac involvement was not different between those with “classic” or mild muscular phenotype. Patients with cardiac involvement had higher ergoreflex sensitivity (24% [8-38] vs. 4% [0-24], p = 0.036), hs-TnI (9.8 ng/L [5.9-14.5] vs. 2.1 ng/L [1.7-4.5], p = 0.018), and hs-TnT (24 ng/L [20-35] vs. 11 ng/L [5-20], p = 0.012), but did not show significant differences in terms of cardiac chamber size or systolic function compared to those without LGE. Ergoreflex sensitivity correlated with biomarkers of both cardiac (hs-TnI, rho = 0.654, p = 0.008; hs-TnT, rho = 0.529, p = 0.043; sST2, rho = 0.654, p = 0.009) and muscular damage (CPK, rho = 0.645, p = 0.009), as well as with the number of LGE segments (rho = 0.583, p = 0.022). No difference in ergoreflex sensitivity was detected in patients with “classic” versus mild muscular phenotype. In BMD, there is no relationship between cardiac involvement and the muscular phenotype. Cardiac involvement in BMD is characterized by enhanced ergoreflex sensitivity, which is associated with biomarkers of both cardiac and muscular damage.

## Abstract
Glucocorticoids (GC) are standard of care treatment in boys with Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD) but are associated with a high incidence of vertebral fractures (VF). Vamorolone (VAM/VBP15) is a dissociative anti-inflammatory steroid with reduced transactivation activity, which may lead to fewer effects on bone.
In a predefined analysis, VF in 39 boys from the VBP15-002/003/LTE (vamorolone) study (NCT03038399) was compared to 70 boys on classic GC from the FOR-DMD study (NCT01603407) matched for common inclusion criteria. In VBP15-LTE, boys initiated VAM at 0.25, 0.75, 2.0, or 6.0 mg/kg/d for 6 months, followed by dose (de)escalations for 2 years. In FOR-DMD, boys were randomized to DEFLAZacortDAILY (0.9mg/kg/d), PREDnisoneDAILY (0.75mg/kg/d) or PREDnisoneINT (0.75mg/kg/d 10 days on/off). VF were read centrally on spine x-rays (Genant method) after 30 (VAM) or 36 (classic GC) months.
At evaluation, mean age on classic GC was 8.9 ± 1,0 and on VAM 8.0 ± 1.0 y. VF prevalence: DEFLAZDAILY 7/23 boys (30.4%) had 18 VF, PREDDAILY 7/26 boys (26.9%) had 11 VF, VAM 4/39 (10.3%; 12.4% adj for shorter duration of LTE study) had 7 VF, PREDINT 0/21.
During the LTE study, the z-scores of the biomarkers for bone formation (osteocalcin and P1NP) and bone resorption (CTX) did not decrease. After 30 months of VAM treatment bone age was 6,8 ± 1,4 y for a calendar age of 8,0 ± 1,0.
In conclusion, after 2.5 years of vamorolone, bone turnover markers were not suppressed, bone age delay was minimal and the VF burden appeared to be lower compared with DFZDaily and PREDDaily
