## Abstract
Daily regimens of prednisone (PDN), deflazacort (DFZ) and vamorolone (VAM) have all been shown to be efficacious in treatment of Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy (DMD) by improving muscle function. The objective of this analysis is to compare the effect of PDN, DFZ and VAM on muscle function after one year of treatment in DMD patients aged 4 to < 7 years at the time of treatment initiation. Data were analyzed from two randomized, double-blind studies (FOR-DMD and VISION-DMD). Two groups from the FOR-DMD study (daily PDN 0.75 m/kg/day and daily DFZ 0.9 mg/kg/day) were compared to patients treated with VAM 6.0 mg/kg/day in the VISION-DMD study. Data between the studies were matched based on propensity scores which were calculated using baseline age, time to stand (TTSTAND) velocity, NSAA score, weight z-score and height z-score. The data on TTSTAND velocity and other measures of motor function were compared using Mixed Model for Repeated Measures (MMRM) with propensity score weighting. Based on the propensity scores, 39 PDN and 47 DFZ treated patients were extracted from the FOR-DMD study and 27 VAM treated patients from the VISION-DMD study. All patients were glucocorticoid naïve at baseline with an average (SD) age of 5.4 (0.6), 5.4 (0.8) and 5.5 (0.9) years for PDN, DFZ and VAM groups, respectively. After 48 to 52 weeks of treatment, the least square mean (LSM) TTSTAND velocity increased in all treatment groups by 0.04 rises/sec, with no significant LSM difference between PDN vs VAM (0.002; 95% CI -0.033 to 0.037; p = 0.929) or DFZ vs VAM (0.002; 95% CI -0.029 to 0.032; p = 0.923). In other efficacy measures (6-minute walk test, NSAA score, time to run/walk 10 meters velocity), comparable increases were seen in all three treatment groups, with no statistically significant differences between PDN vs VAM or DFZ vs VAM. These data show that daily regimens of prednisone, deflazacort and vamorolone have comparable effect in improving motor function in patients with DMD over 1 year of treatment.

## Abstract
Respiratory involvement is less pronounced in Becker muscular dystrophy than in the more severe allelic Duchenne form; however, its evolution over time has been less clearly outlined. Here we present longitudinal forced vital capacity (expressed as percent of predicted by age and height, FVCpp) values from 87 BMD patients followed at our Center.
Average ± standard deviation age at baseline was 33.5 ± 16.3 years, and patients were followed up for an average of 3.5 ± 2.4 pulmonary function evaluations (min-max 1-11). Average FVCpp at baseline was 96.8 ± 21.2%. A linear mixed model did not identify a yearly decrease of FVCpp; in fact, the estimated slope of the FVC trajectory was 0.15 ± 0.14%/year (p = n.s.). Two relevant subpopulations representing a “typical” BMD phenotype, i.e. deletions of exons 45-47 (n = 13, mean age 35.4 ± 13.5 years) and 45-48 (n = 15, mean age 34.9 ± 18.5 years) did not differ from the overall cohort, with baseline FVCpp values of 97.5 ± 15.2 and 95.0 ± 15.0, respectively; and estimated linear slopes of 0.13 ± 0.31%/year, and -0.01 ± 0.23%/year, respectively (p = n.s. for both).
In conclusion, FVCpp does not show a linear decrease in BMD. As respiratory weakness does have some relevance in the more severe BMD subpopulations, more sensitive measures are probably needed for clinical follow-up and as outcome measures for trials.

## Abstract
Glucocorticoid treatment is a recommended standard of care in Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD).
Current recommendations include initiation before the substantial physical decline, typically by the age of 5, using daily dosing of either prednisone 0.75mg/kg/day or deflazacort 0.9mg/kg/day.
The aim of this work was to assess retrospectively the spectrum of changes in the individual’s measures, their correlation with steroid treatment, age at initiation, North Star Ambulatory Assessment (NSAA) and 6-minute walk test (6MWT) values at baseline. A cohort of patients afferent to our hospital, IRCCS Mondino- Pavia, have been assessed at baseline and periodically after baseline using the 6MWT and NSAA which are widely considered as reliable tools in assessing motor evolution of DMD patients.
We have divided the sample according to the age at the introduction of therapy specifically before and after the age of 7 years and by evaluating the initial effect of therapy and long-term efficacy on patients’ motor function. Then we have compared the results of our study with those of the literature. Our findings might help to have a better idea of the trajectory of the disorder depending on therapy introduction timing and initial response.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolDMD in females: a case series of eleven symptomatic carriers
D’AmarioG VeneziaI CordaroG GuidettiC NapoliV FrongiaA L PaneM MercuriE 1Centro Clinico Nemo, Fondazione Policlinico Universitario A. Gemelli, IRCCS, Roma, Italia162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy (DMD) is an X-linked disease and usually affects sons of female asymptomatic carriers. However, some manifesting carriers can present symptoms that vary from mild muscle weakness to more severe clinical features1.
We retrospectively collected data of eleven cases of symptomatic female carriers of DMD followed in our clinical center.
Hereby we present eleven cases of symptomatic Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD) carriers followed at our clinical center. The initial symptoms were hyperckemia and weakness in the 82% of the cases. Cardiac dysfunction was present in 36% of the patients. Cognitive impairment and psychiatric disorders were respectively detected in 55% and 27% of them. Clinical severity and evolution varied from a very mild Becker-like phenotype to Duchenne-like progression.
These case series describe the phenotypic variability in DMD carriers who may present a wide spectrum of disorders ranging from asymptomatic hyperckemia to severe cardiac and cognitive impairment2,3. Muscular weakness and motor involvement can be variable. Due to the high incidence of cardiomyopathy, cardiological screening is recommended. The clinical variability in symptomatic DMD carriers and its molecular basis have to be still clarified.

## Abstract
So far, only few studies reported natural history data about DMD patients carryng small mutations that occur in 25 % of DMD patients. In a recent 36-month longitudinal natural history data using 6MWT in DMD patients with small mutations we found a better overall outcome in DMD boys with small mutations and duplications than in those with deletions, especially in the group of DMD boys above the age of 7 years. The difference between the different types of small mutations (atypical, frameshifting, splicing, nonsense, and missense mutations) was not significant. The aim of this study is to correlate motor functional outcome using the Performance of upper limb (PUL 2.0) and qualitative and quantitative muscle MRI in a cohort of ambulant and not ambulant DMD boys carrying different types of small mutations in order to better understand the genotype and phenotype caracterization. Nine DMD patients with small mutation aged 15.3 ± 4.9 years SD was enrolled: 5 ambulant (mean age: 12.8 ± 3.3 DS years) and 4 not ambulant boys (mean age 18.5 ± 5.1 years SD). On PUL 2.0 not-ambulant group showed lower scores on shoulder and arm domains than the ambulant group with similar results in the distal domain. All patients showed signs of fat replacement with varying degrees of involvement, with an increased muscle impairment in subscapular muscle, followed by infraspinatus, biceps, deltoid, triceps and forearm flexors and extensors muscles, more compromised in not ambulant than ambulant boys. A similar concordance has been found on qualitative and quantitative evaluation.

## Abstract
Despite dilated cardiomyopathy (DCM) is an increasingly frequent cause of death in Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD), genetic components of age at onset and rate of progression have yet to be elucidated. It is recognized that the genetic background may modulate Mendelian disease phenotype: these trans-acting variants are called genetic modifiers. Several modifiers of phenotypic traits of DMD have been identified through candidate gene studies, while genome-wide association study (GWAS) studies have only been initially attempted.
This work represents a preliminary analysis of a GWAS looking for DMD modifier loci of cardiomyopathy, in a cohort of DMD patients followed by Consortium of Tertiary Italian Neuromuscular Centers.
The GWAS was carried out in 240 DMD patients using the high-density Illumina Infinium Omni2.5Exome-8 genotyping chip. The association test was a regression test of echocardiographic measurements of left ventricular ejection fraction (EF) at last visit, with the following covariates: age, glucocorticoid treatment (at least 1 year while ambulatory) and DMD mutation type.
We identified 5 signals represented by a list of SNPs with a p-value close to genome-wide significance.
Cardiomyopathy is a major cause of mortality among DMD patients. The identification of potential genetic modifiers may contribute to refine cardiac prognosis in these patients.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolPoor Bone Health in Patients with Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy
CarraroE MartinelliL PaneM PalermoC LizioA TruccoF AlbamonteE MercuriE SansoneVA 1 NEuroMuscular Omnicentre (NEMO), Fondazione Serena Onlus, Milan, Italy2
University of Milan, School of Medicine, Milan3 NEuroMuscular Omnicentre (NEMO), Fondazione Policlinico Universitario Agostino Gemelli IRCSS, Rome, Italy4 Pediatric Neurology, Università Cattolica del Sacro Cuore, Rome, Italy5 Neurorehabilitation Unit, University of Milan, Milan, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. The article can be used by giving appropriate credit and mentioning the license, but only for non-commercial purposes and only in the original version. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Decreased mobility and long-term corticosteroid therapy are involved in poor bone health in Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy (DMD), but there is no general agreement about the bone treatments.
In this cross-sectional study, we collected data of Dual- energy X-ray Absorptiometry (DXA) on lumbar spine and proximal femur and serum values in genetically confirmed DMD boys.
Thirty subjects (mean age 16.24 years ± 5.08; 12 ambulant and 18 non-ambulant; 8 with pathological fractures) were recruited. Twenty-one patients performed DXA (9 ambulant and 12 non-ambulant), reporting a mean lumbar z-score bone mineral density (BMD) equal to -2.22 ± 1.15 (for ambulant patients) and -2.78 ± 0.85 (for non-ambulant patients) and a mean femoral z-score BMD equal to -3.83 ± 1.66 (for ambulant patients) and -4.24 ± 1.43 (for non-ambulant patients). No significant differences emerged between ambulant and non-ambulant patients in both lumbar spine and proximal femoral BMD; 6 ambulant patients (67%) and 11 non-ambulant patients (92%) reported a z-score < 2 in at least lumbar spine or proximal femoral BMD.
25-hydroxyvitamin D and calcium levels were in range. CTX was analyzed in 15 patients (6 ambulant and 9 non-ambulant), no significant differences emerged in serum values between the two groups. Moreover, 3 ambulant patients (50%) and 4 non-ambulant patients (44%) reported a CTX ≥ 0.584.
DXA seems to be more sensitive than serum exams for bone health in DMD patients. While prevention of osteoporosis is the key (i.e. supplementation of vitamin D), medical treatment is still debatable. We are collecting follow up data about the bone health and therapeutic approaches.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA novel frameshift mutation in HSPB8 gene is responsible for a severe progressive myopathy with cardiac involvement
TudiscoV SframeliM BoscoL GrecoV MateriaR StancanelliC AllegraC RussoM SaidI LentiniA BriugliaS FattoriF RodolicoC MessinaS 1 Department of Clinical and Experimental Medicine, University of Messina, Messina, Italy2 Unit of Neuromuscular Disorders, Bambino Gesù Children’s Hospital, IRCCS, Rome, Italy3 Genetics and Pharmacogenetics Unit, Department of Biomedical and Dental Sciences and Morphofunctional Imaging, University of Messina, Messina, Italy4 Unit of Physical and Rehabilitation Medicine and Sports Medicine, University Hospital “G. ## Abstract
Heat-shock protein B8, encoded by HSPB8 gene, promotes the degradation of damaged cellular proteins, as a component of the chaperone-assisted selective-autophagy complex. Mutations in HSPB8 gene are usually associated to Charcot-Marie-Tooth 2L and distal hereditary motor neuropathy IIa. Recently, rare cases of myofibrillar and rimmed vacuolar myopathies without associated neuropathy have been related to specific mutations in HSPB8. We describe a 31-year-old female with congenital myopathy (CM) with “minicores” diagnosed on muscle biopsy at the age of 14 years, showing a de novo mutation in the HSPB8 gene. She showed the first symptoms at 12years with axial and limb-girdle muscles weakness. The lower limbs weakness progressed leading to loss of ambulation by 25 years. She developed a severe scoliosis, respiratory failure needing non-invasive ventilation by the age of 24 years and associated hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. The creatine-kinase level was normal. Electrodiagnostic studies revealed myopathic changes. Muscle MRI showed fatty degenerative changes of the proximal lower limbs, with vastus lateralis and adductor magnus preferentially affected. Molecular DNA analysis performed on single gene sequencing excluded mutations associated with CM. Recently, the multigenic panel for myopathy revealed a the novo variant: c.580delA(p.Thr194ProfsTer53) in the HSPB8 gene. This mutation is predicted to induce a shifted reading-frame extending the C-terminus of the encoded protein leading to the formation of a longer protein. We report a novel HSPB8 mutation and confirm that frameshift mutations of this gene are associated with myopathies phenotypically overlapping with limb-girdle muscular dystrophies and for the first time a concomitant cardiac involvement is described.

## Abstract
Type 3 SMA 5q presents usually a proximal distribution of muscle weakness, however a distal involvement clinically mimicking a peripheral neuropathy may occur. MCM3AP (minichromosome maintenance complex component 3 associated protein) is an ubiquitously expressed gene encoding an acetyltransferase that acetylates the replication protein and plays a key role in the regulation of DNA replication. Mutations in MCM3AP have recently been reported in literature (18 cases) to cause autosomal recessive Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease (CMT) polyneuropathy. We reported an ambulant 24-year-old-girl with childhood-onset (10 years) of slowly progressive distal and proximal weakness, frequent falls, positive Gower’s sign, toe walking, pes cavus, hypomimia, winged shoulder blades, reduced deep tendon reflexes, fine distal tremor, distal hypotrophy in lower (thighs, prevalent on the left) and upper limbs (intrinsic muscles of the hands), ankle contractures. Cognitive function was normal. CPK values were increased (1000U/L). Electromyography showed reduced cMAP with normal conduction velocities. Muscle MRI documented main adipose infiltration at the great gluteus and proximal anterior district of the thigh and calves. Muscle biopsy showed a neurogenic pattern. Genetic analysis of some forms of distal SMA and motor neuropathy (including TRPV4, HSPB1, HSPB8) and myopathy (lamina A/C, FSHD) was unremarkable. Whole exome sequencing showed a compound heterozygous MCM3AP mutation. MLPA analysis showed a homozygous mutation in SMN1 and 3 copies of SMN2. We discuss the possible role of the combined mutations in SMN1 and MCM3AP into the clinical and instrumental findings of this case. Functional studies will be useful to better understand the pathogenetic mechanism.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolTMEM43 mutations: new variants and different phenotypes
CassandriniD MarchettoG LopergoloD MalandriniA RodolicoC ToninP VattemiG SantorelliFM 1
IRCCS Fondazione Stella Maris, Pisa3 Department of Medicine, Surgery and Neurosciences, University of Siena, Siena2 Department of Neuroscience, Biomedicine and Movement Sciences, University of Verona, Verona4 Department of Clinical and Experimental Medicine, University of Messina, Messina162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
TMEM43 is a protein located in the inner nuclear membrane associated with the linker of the nucleoskeleton and cytoskeleton complex (LINC). Mutations in the TMEM43 gene have been associated with autosomal dominant arrhythmogenic right ventricular dysplasia/cardiomyopathy, less frequently with Emery-Dreifuss muscular dystrophy subtype 7 (EDMD7) and, recently, with auditory neuropathy spectrum disorder (ANSD).
Using a targeted resequencing gene panel and studying the coding exons of 241 “muscle genes”, we analyzed the frequency and type of variants in TMEM43 in patients referred to our neuromuscular and neurogenetic service over the past 5 years.
We found pathogenic or probably pathogenic variants in 4 unrelated individuals. In one case we detected a splice site mutation, while in the other patients two small gene deletions and one deleterious missense variant were found. The clinical presentation of the patients was quite variable. ranging from elevated serum CK levels to a more complex phenotype that did not present as the classic form of EDMD.
Our study confirms that variants in TMEM43 are rare and their interpretation often challenging. We also emphasize the utility of “deep phenotyping” in inherited neuromuscular conditions.

VadiG TorriF BaldinottiF AlìG CenacchiG RicciG SicilianoG 1 Neurology Unit, Department of Clinical and Experimental Medicine, University of Pisa2 Laboratory of Molecular Genetics, University Hospital of Pisa3 Anatomic Pathology Section, Department of Surgical, Medical, Molecular Pathology and Critical Area, University di Pisa4 Department of Biomedical and Neuromotor Sciences, University of Bologna162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Genetic findings might sometimes cause doubts about the most certain diagnosis. We here report the case of a young adult patient who received a diagnosis of OPMD but whose genetic test has raised suspicion of a more complex clinical picture.
A 40-year-old man referred to our clinic for progressive dysphagia to solids. Symptoms onset dated back 6 months before the patient came to our attention and were associated with drooling. Neurologic examination showed mild weakness of lower facial and soft palate muscles, while CK levels, inflammation markers, anti-AchR and anti-Musk antibody were normal. EMG showed a myopathic pattern in all muscles included facial ones. Prednisone was then administered as a “ex juvantibus” criteria but with no significant improvement. Otolaryngological examination with fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation detected a marked impairment of the swallowing act with deficits in oro-pharyngeal musculature and silent aspiration; as a consequence semi-liquid homogeneous diet was indicated with no weight loss reported by the patient. Genetic testing was therefore performed and revealed an abnormal GCN triplet expansion (11 repeats in homozygosis) within the PABPN1 gene. Symptoms continued progressing though, the main complaint of the patient being drooling: scopolamine transdermal patch was unsuccessfully administered and Botox injections into salivary glands were later performed with a partial relief from symptoms. Unexpectedly, genetic test successively also revealed a novel and pathogenic predicted missense mutation (c.G1655A) in LDB3 gene on chromosome 10, which is responsible for autosomal dominant forms of myofibrillar myopathy, a reason why a muscle biopsy is now under way to better characterize genotype-phenotype correlation.

VelardoD AntognozziS RimoldiM PagliaraniS CogiamanianF BarbieriS CortiS ComiG RonchiD 1

## Abstract
Brody Myopathy is a muscle disorder characterized by childhood onset exercise-induced progressive impairment of muscle relaxation, stiffness, cramps, and myalgia, predominantly in four limbs and eyelids. Symptoms generally improve after a few minutes of rest and may be exacerbated by cold. This disorder is recessively inherited and associated with pathogenic variants in the ATP2A1 gene encoding for the Sarco- and Endoplasmic Reticulum Calcium ATPase protein SERCA1.
SERCA1 catalyzes the ATP-dependent uptake of Ca2+ from the cytosol to sarcoplasmic reticulum taking part in the regulation of calcium levels and therefore muscle contraction. In Brody myopathy patients, the activity of SERCA1 in type II muscle fibres is reduced, resulting in delayed muscle relaxation, silent cramps, muscle weakness and muscle atrophy. Since SERCA1 is uniquely expressed in type II skeletal muscle fibres, muscle stiffness in Brody disease is primarily triggered by rapid, intense contractions.
Although about 40 patients from 28 different families have been reported so far our knowledge about disease progression and pathogenesis are far from being exhaustive.
Here we report the clinical, instrumental, and molecular features of two siblings presenting childhood-onset exercise-induced muscle stiffness without pain. Both the probands display difficulty in climbing stairs and running, frequent falls, delayed muscle relaxation after exertion. Cold temperatures worsen these symptoms. No myotonic discharges were observed at electromyography.
Whole Exome Sequencing analysis revealed the presence of two ATP2A1 variants: the previously reported frameshift microdeletion c.2464delC and the likely pathogenic novel splice-site variant c.324+1G > A, whose detrimental effect was demonstrated in ATP2A1 transcript analysis. The bi-allelic inheritance was verified by Sanger sequencing in the unaffected parents. This study expands the molecular defects associated with Brody myopathy.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolClinical and pathological findings in a couple of Italian Gypsy siblings with Charcot-Marie-Tooth type 4D and a review of the current literature
Ferrari AggradiCR CortiS GagliardiD MagriF VelardoD ComiG AbatiE 1Università degli Studi di Milano, Dipartimento di Neurologia del Policlinico di Milano Ospedale Maggiore162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease type 4D (CMT4D) is an early onset autosomal recessive form of CMT characterized by severe demyelinating motor sensory neuropathy, muscle weakness and atrophy leading to gait impairment and foot deformities and hearing impairment. It is caused by mutations in the N-myc downstream-regulated gene 1 (NDRG1). Eight different mutations in NDRG1 have been identified so far. We report the case of two siblings of Romani ancestry, a 38-year-old man and a 40-year-old woman, with a NDRG1 mutation (p.R148) and concurrently provide a review of the current literature. Disease staging was assessed through a battery of clinical and instrumental examinations as CMT neuropathy score, electromyography, visual evoked potentials, fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation of swallowing and pulmonary function tests. The review includes all articles focusing on CMT4D available online, and 15 articles were included. Both patients showed severe distal-proximal motor sensory neuropathy and muscle weakness and atrophy, dysphagia, mild pulmonary restrictive syndrome, and sensorineural deafness. The age at onset, in the first decade of life, and the clinical features are in line with the clinical course described in the literature about this pathogenic variant. p.R148 accounts for the majority of CMT4D cases, almost all of Romani ancestry. Because of the lack of data regarding other pathogenic variants, it is difficult to provide a comparison between potential differences in clinical phenotypes and age at onset. Additional research into NDRG1 mutations and phenotypic characteristics may provide further information about potential genotype-phenotype correlations and prognosis.

CorteseF BrienzaM De GiglioL TodiscoA GaribaldiM FattoriF BernardiC AltavistaMC PennisiEM 1 Medicina, Neurologia, Ospedale San Filippo Neri, Roma, Italia2

## Abstract
We report the case of a 54 year-old- man affected by HCV and HIV infection undergoing antiretroviral therapy, who presented in our outpatient’s department complaining walking difficulties, tendency to fall and fatigue started about 20 years before, with slow progression. He also presented occasional nonspecific exercise myalgia in the lower limb muscles. Blood exams showed a mild elevation of CPK (two-three fold the upper normal limit). On neurological examination, he showed a relevant and bilateral atrophy of the vastus medialis muscle. Strength was mild affected both in the lower limbs (iliopsoas, quadriceps femoral biceps and adductors muscles) and in the upper limbs (triceps and deltoid muscles), with a quite symmetric distribution. Distal muscles were spared.
A muscle MRI showed a relevant fat replacement in the anterior compartment of the thigh, in the posterior compartment of the calf, particularly in the gastrocnemius medialis bilaterally, in the paravertebral muscles and abdominal muscles. Muscle biopsy showed a definite increase of the centralized nuclei, raise of fiber size variability, with presence of some hypertrophic fibers and sporadic necrosis. PAS, Sudan black and histoenzymatic stainings were unremarkable. Sequencing of genes was performed using myopathy panel and a novel heterozygous mutation in BIN1 gene (c.769A > G/ p.SER257Gly) was detected. BIN1 protein plays an important role in organization of T tubules. A dominant centronuclear myopathy caused by heterozygous BIN1 mutations have been described by now only in 5 European families with adult onset myopathy, predominant proximal leg involvement and milder phenotype than the recessive form.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolHACD1-related myopathy with centronuclear myopathy and positive response to L-carnitine supplementation
CapeceG RiguzziP VillaM ZangaroV SogusE PenzoM GorgoglioneD SabbatiniD VianelloS BertolinC BelloL PegoraroE 1Dipartimento di Neuroscienze, Università di Padova, Padova, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Congenital myopathies are a group of inherited muscle diseases characterized by heterogeneous clinical features and traditionally classified according to histologic abnormalities. Abnormal excitation – contraction coupling appears to be implicated into disease pathogenesis. An increasing number of mutations has been identified by the use of next generation sequencing (NGS). Centronuclear myopathy is a type of congenital myopathy characterized by centrally positioned nuclei on muscle biopsy. Muhammad et al. first identified a homozygous nonsense mutation in 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydratase 1 (HACD1) causing congenital myopathy in a Bedouin consanguineous family. The mutation results in a reduction of synthesis of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs), which are involved in stabilization of lipid membranes.
We describe the case of a 59-year-old woman born to consanguineous parents, affected by congenital myopathy, carrying the homozygous mutation c.618dupT p.(Ile207TyrfsTer11) in the HACD1 gene. She first presented with axial muscle weakness in early infancy, with delayed achievement of motor milestones. Over the following years she also developed proximal muscle weakness associated with fatigability. At 11 years of age a muscle biopsy showed several central nuclei, as previously observed in animal models (e.g. HACD1 mutated canines), but not in the other described human patients. She was prescribed L-carnitine supplementation (2gr/die) with subjective improvements in muscular fatigue and weakness.
This is the first case of congenital myopathy caused by mutation in the HACD1 gene with a centronuclear pattern upon muscle biopsy reported so far, which recollects findings in the canine disease and differs from previous human cases. We report a positive response of this patient to L-carnitine supplementation.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolMuscle MRI findings in a pediatric case of congenital fiber type disproportion associated with mutation in TPM3 gene
FuscoS ParraviciniS PaolettiM IoscaA DainesiMI QuarantaCA NicotraR VacchiniV GardaniA GanaS BerardinelliA PichiecchioA 1 Department of Child Neurology and Psychiatry, IRCCS Mondino Foundation, Pavia, Italy2 Department of brain and behavioral sciences, University of Pavia, Pavia, Italy3 Neuroradiology Unit, IRCCS Mondino Foundation, Pavia, Italy4 Medical Genetics Unit, IRCCS Mondino Foundation, Pavia, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Congenital fiber type disproportion (CFTD) is a genetically and clinically heterogeneous disease in which there is relative hypotrophy of type-1-muscle-fibers compared to type-2-fibers on skeletal muscle biopsy.
One of the gene responsible for this congenital myopathy is TPM3 that can causes also nemaline myopathy cap disease and the combination of cap structures and nemaline bodies.
The imaging phenotype on muscle MRI of TPM3-related myopathy is not well established: some reports described the presence of mild and diffuse fatty infiltration without figuring out a specific muscle MRI pattern.
We report a case of a 8-year-old boy with a story of motor developmental delay, hypotonia, upper and lower limb strength deficit, hypomimic and elongated facies. EMG/ENG evidenced myopathic alterations. Muscle biopsy showed characteristic features compatible with definition of CFTD. Molecular analysis confirmed a heterozigous mutation on the TPM3 gene.
At the level of the thigh and pelvic girdle, muscle MRI shows moderate signs of fat replacement of gluteus maximus, hamstring muscles and sartorius, bilaterally. At the level of the lower leg, mild to moderate signs of fat replacement are evident at the level of the anterior compartment and of soleus muscle of the lower leg bilaterally. No signs of muscle edema are evident on STIR.
In conclusion, this is a report of a possible muscle MRI pattern in a pediatric case of congenital fiber type disproportion associated with mutation in TPM3 gene.
Further study of muscle MRI in CFTDs cohorts are needed to better define whether there is a specific disease pattern.

## Abstract
X-linked Myopathy with Excessive Autophagy (XMEA) is a monogenic disorder characterized by a progressive vacuolation and atrophy of skeletal muscles.
XMEA is caused by hypomorphic alleles of the VMA21 gene, resulting in a reduced expression of the Vacuolar ATPase Assembly Factor VMA21 protein, a key proton pump for endo-lysosomal acidification, with consequent impairment of lysosomal acidification, function reduction and finally autophagy failure; from a pathological point of view, this leads to the accumulation of cytoplasmic vacuoles in muscle cells.
Initially described as a childhood-onset, slowly progressive, proximal-predominant myopathy, sparing cardiac and respiratory muscles, the clinical spectrum of XMEA has expanded in the last 10-15 years, with reports of adult-onset cases and the discovery of new pathogenic variants. Nevertheless, comprehensive description of large adult cohorts of XMEA are still missing and data concerning the natural history of the disease is generally lacking.
In this retrospective study, we describe the clinical, electrophysiological, anatomopathological and, when possible, cardio-respiratory examination and radiological presentation of 24 patients with genetically confirmed XMEA, based on a retrospective study of a French cohort, also providing data describing the natural history of this hereditary myopathy. In addition, we carefully revised literature about clinical, histological and electrophysiological reports of the disease.

## Abstract
Mutations in myotilin gene (MYOT) are associated with myofibrillar myopathy and late-onset distal muscular involvement. Given the rarity of this disorder and its challenging identification, a thorough diagnostic workup should include clinical, histological and imaging investigations.
Thus, this study aims at describing such features in the Italian myotilinopathy cohort.
Among the Italian Network of Distal Myopathies, patients with molecular diagnosis of MYOT gene mutations have been selected. Anamnestic and clinical, as well as histological and radiological data have been collected.
Twenty-six subjects (16 males and 10 females), from eight different centers, have been included. All but one carried the typical p.Ser60Phe mutation; only one patient had a novel deletion in MYOT. The mean age at onset was 59.4 (SD 17.5) years old, with weakness and hypotrophy of lower limbs as the most frequent presenting signs and symptoms. In 19 subjects biopsy was performed. Muscle Magnetic Resonance (n = 16) or Computed Tomography (n = 3) showed fatty replacement primarily in leg muscles with greater involvement of posterior compartment.
To the best of our knowledge, this cohort of myotilinopathies is the largest reported in the literature so far. A deeper characterization of these patients will help better identify clinical, histopathological, and radiological features to improve the diagnostic accuracy.

## Abstract
Axial myopathy (AM) is a rare condition characterized by paraspinal muscles weakness causing bent spine (BS) and/or dropped head (DH) which can be observed in different muscle diseases. We retrospectively reviewed the records of the patients who underwent a muscle biopsy at the Neuromuscular Center of our Institution between January 1995 and December 2022, and identified 63 patients (37 females and 26 males) with DH and/or BS as the sole or leading clinical feature. Age at onset was between 23 and 87 years and age at biopsy ranged from 23 to 88 years. A myopathy was diagnosed in forty-thee patients, and a definite diagnosis was reached in twenty of them. Protein aggregate myopathy (PAM) was the most common myopathy identified in our cohort. Additional diagnoses included sporadic inclusion body myositis, granulomatous myositis, central core myopathy, nemaline myopathy, lipid storage myopathy, immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy, Pompe disease, dysferlinopathy, facioscapulohumeral muscular dystrophy, dermatomyositis and mitochondrial myopathy. Our study expands the etiological causes of AM and highlights that axial muscle involvement can be the clinical presentation of a wide spectrum of acquired and hereditary neuromuscular disorders some of which are potentially treatable.

## Abstract
Hyperckemia is one of the most common causes of consultation in a neuromuscular outpatient clinic, both in childhood and later ages. As reported in literature, hyperckemia underlies many different causes.
It’s well known that CNS involvement can be even a relevant feature in some NMDs, such as CMDs, but of course it’s also possible that a primitive CNS involvement is associated to a NMDs or even a different condition can explain both hyperckemia and CNS involvement, making harder the diagnosis.
We report the case of a 2-year-old girl with significative hyperckemia (1048 U/L), spastic tetraparesis and ocular abnormalities, with reduced visual acuity and contrast sensitivity, feeding and sleep problems since birth, and impaired motor and language development, born to healthy, non-consanguineous parents and without family history of NMDs.
We will detail the diagnostic workout, including EMG, brain ultrasound, MR and EEG and molecular analysis by discussing the possible differential diagnosis.
The final diagnosis is an uncommon condition that should be kept in mind when facing peculiar hyperckemias.

RiguzziP BelloL FustoA VillaM ZangaroV CapeceG TripodiS MinerviniG SumanM VianelloS AgostoA SorarùG BertolinC NigroV TosattoS CenacchiG StramareR ReggianiC PegoraroE 1 ERN Neuromuscular Center, Department of Neuroscience, University of Padova, Italy2 Department of Biomedical Sciences, University of Padova, Italy3 Pediatric Pain and Palliative Care Service, Department of Women’s and Children’s Health, Padua University Hospital, Italy4 Department of Precision Medicine, University of Campania “Luigi Vanvitelli”, Telethon Institute of Genetics and Medicine (TIGEM) Naples, Italy5 Department of Biomedical and Neuromotor Sciences, Alma Mater Studiorum University of Bologna, Bologna, Italy6 Department of Medicine DIMED, Institute of Radiology, Padova University Hospital, Padova, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Primitive triadopathies are rare neuromuscular diseases caused by structural and functional alterations of various actors at the muscle triads. STAC3-related congenital myopathy and Tubular Aggregate Myopathy (TAM) are due to mutations in key players at the triads: STAC3, STIM1 and ORAI1.
We selected 9 patients from the Neuromuscular Center of Padova database. The identified mutations were modeled in silico and further studied in vitro in dissected single fiber. Clinical features and muscle MRI were longitudinally evaluated. Patients’ muscle biopsies were characterized at the histopathological level and using a panel of antibodies directed against various component of the triad.
We identified a patient carrying a novel homozygous loss of function STAC3 mutation, a three generation family segregating the known p.Leu92Val STIM1 mutation, and 2 patients carrying two novel ORAI1 mutations. Both STIM1 and ORAI1 gene mutations resulted in a tubular aggregates (TA) myopathy. In silico modeling of STIM1 and ORAI1 mutations predicted a dominant gain of function mechanism resulting in constitutive Ca2+ influx, while in vitro studies in single muscle fibers showed a profound alteration of fiber morphology with some adaptive changes and decreased isometric voltage in STIM1 fibers. Muscle MRI confirmed a specific pattern of muscle involvement in STIM1-TAM. Finally, clinical phenotype in the STAC3-mutated patient was consistent with a severe congenital myopathy with multi-systemic features.
Deep characterization of larger cohorts of patients carrying mutations at different players at the triad level will help clarify the molecular mechanisms and possibly identify therapeutic targets for this emerging group of diseases.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolNovel PIEZO2 variants are responsible for a very suggestive clinical phenotype in early childhood
DiodatoD CatterucciaM SancesarioA TosiM BoscoL FattoriFDe LucaG MizzoniI CarlesiA BertiniE D’AmicoA 1 Muscular and Neurodegenerative Disease Unit, Ospedale Pediatrico Bambino Gesù, Roma, Italia2 Neurorehabilitation Unit, Ospedale Pediatrico Bambino Gesù, Roma, Italia3 Medical Genetics, Ospedale Pediatrico Bambino Gesù, Roma, Italia4 Functional Rehabilitation Unit, Ospedale Pediatrico Bambino Gesù, Roma, Italia162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Mechanosensation is the ability to detect dynamic mechanical stimuli and is essential for many processes, including our sense of touch on the skin. PIEZO2 is a functional ion channel assembled from three PIEZO1 monomers and is an essential mechanotransducer for touch, proprioception, and interoception. Heterozygous pathogenic variants in PIEZO2 gene are associated with distal arthrogryposis type 3 (MIM:114300) and type 5 (MIM:108145), and in one case with Marden-Walker Syndrome (MIM:248700). Recessive pathogenic variants in PIEZO2 are associated with distal arthrogryposis with impaired proprioception and touch (DAIPT) (MIM:617146). Papers describing patients’ cohorts in literature are scarce. Here we described 5 patients from 4 families with a very similar clinical picture characterized by neonatal respiratory distress, hypotonia, delayed motor development, sensory ataxia, foot deformities, hyperlaxity, progressive scoliosis and skeletal contractures. Clinical picture is variable in severity. In all patients electroneurography showed a mild sensory axonal neuropathy. In our cohort we found seven PIEZO2 variants not previously reported in literature and not described in public databases. All patients harbour compound heterozygous PIEZO2 variants, except for one patient, presenting a very suggestive clinical phenotype, in which we found only one frameshift variant. Compound heterozygous variants segregate in the parents. Here we describe 5 patients with novel PIEZO2 variants. The clinical phenotype, even though of variable severity, is highly concordant between the patients. Clinical picture at birth of respiratory insufficiency, laryngeal stridor, foot deformities, hyperlaxity, mild sensory axonal neuropathy should be considered as highly suggestive of recessive PIEZO2 defect.

CumboF TosiM CarlesiA MizzoniI CatterucciaM BertiniES D’AmicoA 1Unit of Muscular and Neurodegenerative Disorders, Bambino Gesù Children’s Hospital, IRCCS, Rome, Italy1 Developmental Neurology Unit, Bambino Gesù Children’s Hospital, IRCCS, Rome, Italy* These authors contributed equally

## Abstract
XLMTM1 is a severe congenital myopathy, potentially fatal within the first years. Patients present several complications and their cognitive evaluation has never been explored deeply so far. A deep knowledge of the disease natural history, including the neurocognitive and adaptive profile, is essential in light of the promising new therapeutic perspectives.
We enrolled all XL-MTM1 patients in clinical follow-up in our centre, irrespective to the disease’s severity. Demographic and clinical data, including motor, respiratory and swallowing functions were collected. Patients were assessed with gold-standard international scales, according to their age and communication skills. Most patients had a significant motor impairment (n = 2 had no head control; n = 3 were able to sit, n = 1 was able to walk), respiratory difficulties (tracheostomy 37.5%, NIV 25%) and dysphagia (PEG 87.5%). Patients < 6 years old (n = 4) scored a global Griffiths-III at the lower limits of the norm (mean 79.3). Patients > 6 years old (n = 3), were assessed with the Coloured Progressive Matrices and scored lower than the norm (median 3.5). A global adaptive deficit was revealed using the Vineland-II scale (mean 56.5). Some patients presented perseverative behaviours, the specific evaluation scales CARS and CY-BOCS were respectively in the norm and borderline.
This study shows an overall adequate neurodevelopmental profile, while the adaptive difficulties seem to be related to patients’ global clinical impairment. It could be interesting to explore in depth these aspects in future studies including the neurocognitive development in the natural history of XL-MTM1, also considering the incoming promising therapeutic approaches.

## Abstract
Ullrich congenital muscular dystrophy and Bethlem myopathy are no longer considered separate disorders, but the two ends of the clinical spectrum of collagen VI-related myopathies.
They are due to autosomal dominant or recessive mutations in genes coding for the alpha chains of collagen VI, an important component of the extracellular matrix of several tissues, with biomechanical and cytoprotective functions.
The phenotype includes proximal muscle weakness, joint hyperlaxity, contractures with possible toe walking, rigid spine and restrictive respiratory insufficiency. Skin changes are also associated, placing these conditions in differential diagnosis with other connectivopathies.
The diagnostic process includes collagen VI immunolabeling of muscle tissue and dermal fibroblasts and peculiar muscle MRI findings, and is complemented by genetic testing.
However, also in early-onset pediatric cases, the finding of genetic variants is combined with inconclusive findings, especially in the intermediate and Bethlem forms, so there is increasing interest in describing the variety of early phenotypes and in combining multiple diagnostic tools.
The aim of this work, therefore, is to describe 7 pediatric cases of collagen VI myopathies, analyzing their clinical features at onset and during the course of disease, with an average follow-up of 8 years. Differential features and the diagnostic course with biopsy and muscle MRI findings will be highlighted.
The clinical description not only provides a basis for early genotype-phenotype correlations, which will be important in the perspective of a possible gene therapy, but it also helps to delineate a trajectory of motor and pulmonary function, essential for rehabilitation project.

## Abstract
Congenital myopathies are a group of clinically, genetically and histologically heterogeneous diseases caused by mutations in a large group of genes. One of these was CACNA1S which is recognized as the cause of Dihydropyridine Receptor Congenital Myopathy.
To better characterize the phenotypic spectrum of CACNA1S myopathy, we conducted a systematic review of cases in the literature through three electronic databases following PRISMA guidelines. We selected 9 articles describing 23 patients with heterozygous, homozygous, or compound heterozygous mutations in CACNA1S and we added one patient with compound heterozygous mutation in CACNA1S (c.1394-2A > G; c.1724T > C, p.L575P) followed at our Institute. We collected clinical and genetic data, muscle biopsy and muscle MRI when available.
The phenotype of this myopathy is heterogeneous, ranging from more severe forms with lethal early onset and mild-moderate forms with better clinical course. Our patient presented a phenotype compatible with the mild-moderate form, although she presented peculiar features such as short stature, myopia, mild sensorineural hearing loss, psychiatric symptoms and posterior-anterior impairment gradient on thigh muscles MRI.

## Abstract
Collagen VI is an extracellular matrix component encoded by COL6A1, COL6A2 and COL6A3 genes. Causative variants in these genes are associated with Collagen VI-Related Dystrophy (COL6-RD): the severe Ullrich Congenital Muscular Dystrophy (UCMD), the milder Bethlem Myopathy (BM) and Intermediate phenotypes (INT).
In the period 2007-2022 the Medical Genetics Unit in Ferrara identified Collagen VI variants in 153 Italian patients referred for a COL6-RD. The analysis was either performed by Sanger sequencing or Next Generation Sequencing (NGS) approaches.
We identified 46 (30.1%) UCMD patients (loss of ambulation < 12years), 10 (6,5%) INT patients (loss of ambulation > 13 < 19 years) and 71 (46,4%) BM patients (loss of ambulation > 19 years). We also considered an INT/BM category of patients still able to walk at the last clinical evaluation, performed between 12 and 19 years (26 patients, 17%).
We identified 191 total variants: 53 in COL6A1 (27,8%), 87 in COL6A2 (45,5%) and 51 in COL6A3 (26,7%). The variant spectrum encompasses missense, splicing, small indel and nonsense variants. The glycine substitutions in the triple helical domain of the protein are the most common mutations occurring in all phenotypic classes. For example, we found the COL6A1 p.Gly293Arg in 6 patients (1 UCMD, 2 INT/BM, 1 INT and 2 BM) and the p.Gly284Arg in 9 patients (6 UCMD, 2 INT/BM, 1 BM).
Defining the causative variant profile and genotype/phenotype relationships in a large cohort of patients with COL6-RD will be useful for improving diagnosis, for modifiers studies and for future personalised therapies.

## Abstract
Core myopathies are congenital muscle diseases characterized by clinical, pathological and genetic heterogeneity. The main feature at skeletal muscle biopsy is the presence of “cores” showing a variable myofibrillar disruption and focally reduced oxidative enzyme activity, which indicates lack of mitochondria. Core myopathies are traditionally classified in Central Core Disease, Multi-minicore Disease, Core-rod myopathy. Dusty Core Disease (DuCD) is the last Core Myopathy recently identified, that differs in the presence of irregular areas, without clear borders and round/ovoidal shape. The myofibrillar disorganization is characterized by the reddish-purple granular material deposition, that shows patchy oxidative staining, and by absence of ATPase activity. Until now, DuCD was associated to biallelic recessive RYR1 mutations. RYR1 encodes the ryanodine receptor channel1, a sarcoplasmic reticulum calcium channel that interacts with DHPR (dihydropyridine receptor) in the T-tubule. This interaction is essential for the excitation-contraction coupling of the skeletal muscle. The aim of this work was to analyze the clinical aspects, pathological features and mutational spectrum of 4 RYR1 mutated patients with histological and immunological features of Dusty Core Disease. Patients were selected from a cohort of probands, affected with Congenital Myopathy, who underwent Next Generation Sequencing analysis. Patients presented a heterogeneous phenotype that ranged from severe forms characterized by childhood onset to milder adult-onset presentations. The genetic analyses allowed the identification of 4 novel mutations out of 6 variants, localized in important structural and functional protein domains. Different histological, immunological and ultrastructural techniques allowed to validate the new genetic variants.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolCOLQ-related congenital myasthenic syndrome: a clinical and genetic report
OlivieriE RisiB Cotti PiccinelliS CariaF DamioliS LabellaB FerulloL PoliL Alessandro PadovaniA FilostoM 1 Department of Clinical and Experimental Sciences, University of Brescia, Brescia, Italy2
Unit of Neurology, ASST Spedali Civili, Brescia, Italy3
NeMO-Brescia Clinical Center for Neuromuscular Diseases, Brescia, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Congenital Myasthenic Syndromes (CMS) are a clinically and genetically heterogeneous group of disorders due to gene mutations impairing neuromuscular transmission.
The COLQ gene encodes a triple-stranded collagenic tail anchoring catalytic subunits of acetylcholinesterase to the basal lamina. Mutations in COLQ cause the Congenital Myasthenic Syndrome-5 (CMS5).
A 23-year-old man was evaluated following a long history of asthenia and muscle weakness. In his childhood he developed axial and shoulder girdle hypotonia and weakness that repeatedly worsened concurrently with episodes of severe asthenia and respiratory failure. Easy fatigue and deficient cough were also noted. Over the years, his picture has globally improved save the easy fatigue, which does not significantly impact daily life.
Two muscle biopsies at the age of 1 and 10 showed non-specific myogenic changes. No family history of neurological diseases was reported.
Neurological examination showed bilateral eyelid ptosis, mild facial weakness, moderate limb-girdle and axial muscle weakness and hypotrophy associated with early muscle exhaustion.
A decremental response to repetitive nerve stimulation was detected.
Targeted NGS analysis displayed the homozygote missense variant c.1321A > G (p.Thr441Ala) in the COLQ gene, classified as pathogenetic. Both parents were heterozygous for the same mutation.
CMS are very rare conditions and diagnostic delays are frequent. Our patient was diagnosed at 23 despite suggestive symptoms since early childhood.
It is essential to include CMS in the diagnostic process of myopathies so as to speed up diagnosis, especially when an effective symptomatic treatment is available, such as for COLQ-related syndrome.

## Abstract
Efgartigimod, a human IgG1 antibody Fc-fragment, reduces total IgG and pathogenic IgG autoantibody levels through neonatal Fc receptor blockade. ADAPT was a 26-week, global, multicenter, randomized, controlled, phase 3 trial evaluating efgartigimod in patients with generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG). Patients completing ADAPT were eligible for the open-label extension study, ADAPT+, which evaluated long-term safety and efficacy of efgartigimod.
Efgartigimod (10 mg/kg IV) was administered in cycles of 4 weekly infusions, with subsequent cycles initiated based on clinical evaluation. Efficacy was assessed utilizing Myasthenia Gravis Activities of Daily Living (MG-ADL) and Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis (QMG) scales.
151/167 patients from ADAPT entered ADAPT+. 145 (111 anti-AChR-Ab+/34 anti-AChR-Ab-) patients received ≥1 cycle as of January 2022. With 217.5 patient-years of follow-up (mean duration per patient: 548 days), the most common adverse events (mostly mild-moderate) were headache (25%), concomitant COVID-19 infection (15%), and nasopharyngitis (14%), which did not increase in frequency with subsequent cycles.
AChR-Ab+ patients with ≥1 year of follow-up across ADAPT/ADAPT+ (n = 95) received a median (range) 5.0 (0.4–7.6) cycles per year. Anti-AChR-Ab+ patients demonstrated consistent improvements in MG-ADL (mean[SE] change week 3 of cycle 1: -5.0[0.33]; up to 14 cycles) and QMG (-4.7[0.41]; up to 7 cycles) scores during each cycle, mirroring repeatable reductions in total IgG (mean[SE] reduction: -55.9%[1.15]; up to 7 cycles) and anti-AChR autoantibody levels (-56.1%[1.43]; up to 7 cycles).
Long-term efgartigimod treatment was well-tolerated and resulted in consistent, repeatable reductions in IgG antibody levels and clinical outcomes in patients with gMG.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolUsefulness and reliability of a self-monitoring diary for the management of patients with Myasthenia Gravis
StragliatiF NurediniA AnceschiP RomanoS TinchelliS ChiericiE E SaccaniE 1Dipartimento di Medicina e Chirurgia, Università degli Studi di Parma1 UOC di Neurologia, Ambulatorio Malattie Neuromuscolari, Dipartimenti di Emergenza-Urgenza ed Area Medica Generale e Specialistica, Azienda Ospedaliero-Universitaria di Parma162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Myasthenia Gravis (MG) is a chronic autoimmune neuromuscular junction disease, with fluctuating muscular weakness that varies greatly throughout the day. Therefore, patient-reported symptoms and impact may provide more precise information on disease severity than clinical evaluation alone.
The aim of this stsudy was to assess the reliability and usefulness of a self-monitoring daily diary and its validity compared to calculated MG score and MGQoL15.
We enrolled 17 MG patients (8 males), 11 affected by gMG and 6 ocular MG, confirmed by anti-AChR antibody positivity and/or electrodiagnostic tests. The mean age was 59,1 years, mean disease duration was 5,1 years. Patients were evaluated using MG score and MGQoL15 at the time of enrolment.
The daily diary included items on type and severity of symptoms and their impact on quality of life. Patients were asked to fill the diary for three months, and a satisfaction survey was administered at the end of the study.
Compliance was 94,1%. Patients reported the completion of the diary as easy and no time consuming; 93,7% felt more medically monitored. There was a positive correlation between MG score and the self-reported number of days with any severe symptom, with any symptom but at least two bulbar symptoms, with any severe symptom but at least two bulbar symptoms, with motor symptoms. Self-reported impact on quality of life was found to correlate with MGQoL15 scores.
As reported for other chronic diseases, a self-monitoring daily diary is a valid tool for measuring patient-reported symptoms severity and its impact on quality of life.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolImmune checkpoint inhibitors-induced Myasthenia Gravis: the need to stratify patients
ScarsiE MassuccoS FaedoE GemelliC GarneroM GenovaC TandaET Delle PianeC BenedettiL SchenoneA GrandisM 1 IRCCS Policlinico San Martino Hospital, University of Genoa, DINOGMI, Genoa, Italia2

## Abstract
Immune checkpoint inhibitors (ICIs) are increasingly used in the treatment of malignancies due to their proven efficacy. ICIs are associated with immune-related adverse effects (irAEs) including Myasthenia Gravis (MG). Most cases develop by the fifth dose of treatment. Management of these complications requires stopping the ICIs and, in some cases, treating them with immunomodulant or immunosuppressive drugs. In Liguria we have observed several cases of Myasthenia Gravis onset after initiation of ICIs, often with a rapid course and poor outcomes.
We are collecting demographic, oncological, and neurological data to stratify them and identify patients who could benefit from more aggressive immunomodulatory treatment. We are including patients with a confirmed diagnosis of malignancy, treated with ICIs and with a subsequent clinical diagnosis of MG possibly associated with myositis/myocarditis. Patients with a pre-existing diagnosis of MG are excluded.
We are collecting data on these cases and comparing them with the literature to evaluate which factors correlate with a more severe course.
Stratifying patients based on the oncological prognosis and the severity of the neurological complication is essential to evaluate the best treatment. Early use of intravenous immunoglobulins or plasma exchange may lead to better outcomes than steroids alone, but in some cases the clinical response is poor. There are reports of cases treated effectively with CD20 inhibitors, however with long latency of effect. Now, the decision is mainly based on disease severity at presentation. It would be important to identify an algorithm to decide which patients to reserve the most aggressive therapies.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolCancer frequency in muscle-specific tyrosine kinase (MuSK) myasthenia gravis
FalsoS MariniS SabatelliE EvoliA IorioR 1 Neuroscience Department, Catholic University of the Sacred Heart, Rome, Italy2
Neurology Institute, Fondazione Policlinico Gemelli IRCCS, Rome, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Cancer frequency in myasthenia gravis with anti-MuSK antibodies (MuSK-MG) has not yet been explored. The link between neurological autoimmunity and cancer is bidirectional: autoimmune diseases may increase cancer risk through long-term immunosuppression, conversely antitumor immune response may result in autoimmunity development.
In this retrospective study, we reviewed records of patients with confirmed diagnosis of MuSK-MG who had been followed for at least 1 year from disease onset in our Center. We recorded associated cancers and timing of oncological diagnosis in relation to MG onset, type and duration of immunosuppressive therapy.
94 patients were recruited, 21 males and 73 females. Median age at MG onset was 51 years [19-75]. Immunosuppressive therapy was performed in 90/94 (95.7%) cases with corticosteroids and/or immunosuppressants; 16 patients were treated with Rituximab.
Fifteen cancers occurred in 13/94 patients (13.8%). Median age at cancer onset was 60 years [18-79]. Tumor detection preceded MG onset (median: 11 years, [3-17]) in 5 patients, it was concomitant in 2 and it followed MG diagnosis in 8 cases (median from MG onset: 11.5 years, [1-32]). Six patients were on long-term immunosuppression at cancer diagnosis. Blood malignancies were the most common (N = 5), all detected before MG onset. In addition, we found cancer in breast (N = 3), womb (N = 2), digestive organs (N = 2), lung (N = 1), vocal cords (N = 1), skin (N = 1).
Onco-hematological diseases, particularly mediastinal lymphoma (N = 3/5, 60%), were the most common malignancy detected in MuSK-MG patients. We did not find a higher frequency of cancer occurrence in patients on long-term immunosuppression.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolEculizumab for Immune Checkpoint Inhibitors (ICIs)-related Myasthenia Gravis
RossiniE LaulettaA MarchettiP AntoniniG MorinoS GaribaldiM FiondaL 1 NESMOS Department, Sapienza University of Rome, Italy2 Department of Clinical and Molecular Medicine, Medical Oncology Unit, Sant’Andrea Hospital, Sapienza University, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Myasthenia gravis (MG) is the most common autoimmune disorder of the neuromuscular junction. Beyond classical therapies, complement inhibitors represent a new frontier of treatment, especially in patients with multiple comorbidities that cannot receive steroids and other immunosuppressive agents.
Herein we report on a case of a 76-year-old male affected by generalized anti-AchR positive MG (MGFA IIb) successfully treated for 6 years with prednisone and azathioprine. He also received a diagnosis of metastatic colorectal cancer at the age of 74 unsuccessfully treated with surgery and first-line chemotherapy. For this reason, an ICI (Pembrolizumab) was started with remarkable tumor remission. After the first two Pembrolizumab infusions, MG symptoms relapsed showing complete bilateral ptosis and ophthalmoplegia, fatigable dysarthria and swallowing impairment (MGADL 14, QMG 14). IVIG (2 gr/kg) was started with mild clinical improvement. Therefore, a third line therapy with complement inhibitor (Eculizumab) was started. After the first two infusions, MGADL and QMG improved to 10 and 12 respectively.
Pembrolizumab is a humanized monoclonal immunoglobulin IgG4 antibody directed against human cell surface receptor PD-1 (programmed cell death-1), an inhibitory signalling receptor expressed on the surface of activated T cells and blocks the binding to and activation of PD-1 by its ligands, which results in the activation of T-cell-mediated immune responses against tumor cells. Complement inhibition performed by Eculizumab does not interfere with its mechanism of action, while its efficacy in anti-AchR generalized MG is proved.
Personalized medicine through molecular pathways targeting can help to achieve therapeutic goals in patients with multiple comorbidities.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolClinical and genetic heterogeneity in Congenital Myasthenic Syndromes: a pediatric case series
TrentinF SalceB ScarpiniG GiannottaM NoceraGM PastorelliF SantorelliFM PalomboF ValentinoML FrattiniD FuscoC Di PisaV CordelliDM PiniA 1 UO Neuropsichiatria dell’Età Pediatrica, IRCCS Istituto delle Scienze Neurologiche di Bologna, Italy2 Unità Neuromuscolare dell’Età Evolutiva, IRCCS Istituto delle Scienze Neurologiche di Bologna, Italy3

## Abstract
Congenital myasthenic syndromes (CMS) are a diverse group of rare neuromuscular disorders. Diagnostic clues of CMS arise from a combination of clinical, instrumental, and genetic findings. However, diagnostic process can be challenging because neurophysiological studies can be normal and clinical pictures heterogeneous. Therefore, genetic testing is necessary to obtain the diagnosis. Mutations responsible for CMS have been identified in many genes coding for neuromuscular junction (NMJ) components, associated with variable clinical phenotypes, age of onset, progression, response to therapy. A correct genetic diagnosis is crucial to enable the more effective pharmacological treatment. We report a series of 7 pediatric patients, including two siblings, affected by CMS caused by different genes (AGRN, COLQ, RAPSN, SLC5A7, DOK7, GFPT1) focusing on their clinical and genetic differences. The age of onset was in the first three years of life. In the AGRN muted case, the main clinical findings at onset were autistic features and neurodevelopmental delay; in SLC5A7, COLQ, RAPSN and DOK7– related patients there was a congenital onset with palpebral ptosis, ophtalmoplegia, respiratory failure, with the DOK7 mutant case presenting a milder progression and the RAPSN mutant case having a better response to therapy. Two siblings harbored variants in GFPT1: the eldest brother showed myopathy and fatigability, whereas the youngest brother presented autistic features. Whilst initial NMJ involvement was unremarkable, we disclosed a significant decrease at repetitive nerve stimulation once a genetic diagnosis had been reached. Consequently, a more precise pharmacological treatment was possible. Our cohort is an example of the heterogeneous presentation of CMS and confirms the essential role of genetic testing.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolLambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome (LEMS) following SARS-CoV2 infection.
BarbacciaA PuglieseA BiasiniF AndreettaF MiglioratoA RodolicoC 1 Department of Clinical and Experimental Medicine, University of Messina, Messina, Italy2
Neurological Institute “Carlo Besta”, Milan, Italy3 Department of Biomedical and Dental Sciences and Morpho-Functional imaging, University of Messina, Italy Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome (LEMS) is a rare paraneoplastic or primary autoimmune neuromuscular junction disorder, due to antibodies directed against the presynaptic P/Q-type voltage-gated calcium channels (VGCC-Ab)162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
We describe, herein, an 80-year-old male patient who acutely presented fatigability, proximal muscle weakness, swallowing difficulty, and autonomic dysfunction (dry eyes and mouth, and bradycardia). Symptoms occurred two months after a symptomatic SARS-CoV2 infection. Neurological examination revealed waddling gait with steppage and more evident limb-girdle muscles weakness, areflexia with post-exercise facilitation. Electrodiagnostic studies detected a low cMAP amplitude recorded at rest from the abductor quintii digiti muscle, followed by a clear incremental response after a brief exercise. VGCC-Ab were negative. Total body CT scan and PET excluded any malignancy. Treatment with prednisone (25 mg/day) and 3-4 diaminopyridine phosphate was started (40 mg/day), with a striking clinical improvement. After one month of therapy, patient showed dysautonomia improvement and no muscle weakness.
To date this is the first case of LEMS occurring after SARS-CoV2 infection. We suppose that SARS Cov2 infection could have triggered the autoimmune disease. We suggest consider also LEMS diagnosis in patients with recent SARS Cov2 infection, fatigability and proximal weakness.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolRavulizumab for the treatment of generalized Myasthenia Gravis: timing of response

The University of North Carolina, Chapel Hill, NC, USATo assess the timing of first response to ravulizumab in patients with generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG) in the CHAMPION MG-306 study162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
The phase 3 CHAMPION MG-306 study demonstrated that ravulizumab is an effective and well-tolerated treatment for anti-acetylcholine receptor antibody-positive (AChR Ab+) gMG. Endpoints for primary data analysis were assessed at Week 26 of the randomized controlled period (RCP); responses at earlier timepoints have not been examined.
Data were included from patients with a Myasthenia Gravis-Activities of Daily Living (MG-ADL) total score of ≥6 at ravulizumab initiation who received ravulizumab in the RCP or in the open-label extension. Cumulative MG-ADL response rates at various timepoints after ravulizumab initiation were evaluated. Time to first response (improvement in MG-ADL total score ≥3 points) after ravulizumab initiation was also assessed, using the Kaplan–Meier product-limit method.
The analysis included 139 patients who were treated with ravulizumab for up to 60 weeks. Cumulative proportions of patients achieving MG-ADL response by Weeks 2, 4, 12, 26, and 60 were 45.3%, 55.4% 66.2%, 75.5%, and 82.0%, respectively. Median (interquartile range) time to first MG-ADL response was 29 (13-183) days.
Cumulative response rates indicate that first response was achieved by almost half of patients after one ravulizumab infusion (by Week 2) and by two-thirds of patients by Week 12; some patients (15.8%) achieved a first response after longer treatment. Median time to first response to ravulizumab treatment in patients with AChR Ab+ gMG was approximately 4 weeks.
The randomized controlled period of the CHAMPION MG phase 3 study demonstrated that ravulizumab administered every 8 weeks was associated with rapid improvements in symptoms (vs placebo) that were sustained through 26 weeks in adult patients with anti-acetylcholine receptor antibody positive (AChR Ab+) generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG). Interim analysis of the ongoing open-label extension demonstrated the sustained (up to 60 weeks) efficacy of ravulizumab in reducing MG symptoms. An analysis has now been conducted to assess in greater detail the timing of first clinically meaningful response to ravulizumab. Data from the CHAMPION MG study demonstrated that ravulizumab is effective in reducing MG symptoms in adults with AChR Ab+ gMG. Greater detail on timing of initial clinically meaningful response provides information on how soon after treatment initiation a response may be achieved and thus aids disease-management decisions.

BruschiF ArdissoneA CianoC AntozziC MantegazzaR MoroniI 1 Department of Pediatric Neurosciences, Fondazione IRCCS Istituto Neurologico Carlo Besta, Milan2 Neurophysiology Unit, Fondazione IRCCS Istituto Neurologico Carlo Besta, Milan3 Neuroimmunology and Neuromuscular Diseases Unit, Fondazione IRCCS Istituto Neurologico Carlo Besta, Milan4 Department of Biomedical and Clinical Sciences “Luigi Sacco”, University of Milan, Milan162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Juvenile myasthenia gravis (JMG) is a rare disease and evidence-based guidelines are still lacking. JMG may present with purely ocular (OMG) or more generalized muscle involvement (GMG).
This study aims to provide an in-depth clinical characterization of a cohort of 41 patients (15M,26F) who have been followed-up (FU) over the last 20 years. Clinical data were collected retrospectively.
In the 18 OMG (9M,9F) patients, mean age at onset was 8 ± 4.2 years. 50% of patients were positive for nicotinic acetylcholine receptor (AChR) antibodies. Repetitive nerve stimulation (RNS) was performed in 15/18 and showed decremental response in 60%. Treatment was based on pyridostigmine (Py) only in 9/18, Py and glucocorticoids (GC) in 7/18. One patient received a single IVIg course and one remitted spontaneously. At last FU complete remission under medical treatment was obtained in 7/18 and 3/18 were asymptomatic and drug-free.
Within the GMG population (23 patients; 6M,17F), mean age at onset was 9.8 ± 4.2 years; 47% presented with bulbar signs. 61% had positive AChR antibodies, 17% muscle-specific kinase antibodies, and 22% were seronegative. RNS showed decremental response in 87%. Treatment was based on Py only in 7; Py and GC in 3; Py, GC and IVIg in 7 patients; immunosuppressants and plasmapheresis were required in 6 and 3 patients respectively. Thymectomy was performed in 8/23. At last FU, complete remission under medical treatment was obtained in 34.7% of patients.
Treatment-refractory JMG is rare, but it represents a serious challenge for child neurologists and an unmet need for effective, well-tolerated therapies.

Collection date 2023 Jun.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolA triple seronegative, refractory patient with Generalized Myasthenia Gravis successfully managed with Efgartigimod
FalzoneYM SorrentiB LauriniC StranoC BoscoL SchitoP ScarlatoM FazioR FilippiM PrevitaliS 1Neuromuscular Repair Unit, InSpe and Division of Neuroscience, IRCCS Ospedale San Raffaele162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. For further information: https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc-nd/4.0/deed.en

## Abstract
Myasthenia gravis (MG) is an autoimmune disease of the neuromuscular junction in which autoantibodies disrupt the physiological nerve-muscle crosstalk1. 10-15% of patients test negative for anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) and anti-muscle specific kinase (MuSK) antibodies1. A variable percentage of patients are refractory to several treatments requiring either IVIg or PLEX2. The therapeutic landscape for anti-AChR gMG is close to be revolutionized by novel biological drugs, while seronegative MG is far to be considered eligible for these treatments3. Herein we present a 56-year-old woman with a 28-year course of triple seronegative, refractory, gMG successfully managed with Efgartigimod.
At the age of 28, our patient was diagnosed with seronegative gMG on the basis of clinical symptoms and neurophysiological assessment. Radioimmunoassay tested negative for AChR, MuSK and anti-LRP4 autoantibodies. Multiple myasthenic crises occurred throughout the years despite several lines of treatments, therefore non-invasive ventilation and monthly PLEX were started achieving an amelioration of symptoms but many complications occurred. Three cycles of Efgartigimod were administered between October 2022 and March 2023 with a clearcut fast symptom relief. In the last six months, PLEX was needed only once between the first and second cycle then we decided to reduce the Efgartigimod treatment-free period from the approved four weeks to three weeks achieving a complete disease control.
This case describes a triple seronegative, refractory gMG successfully treated with Efgartigimod. Our report strengthens the hypothesis that Efgartigimod can be a valid option in the management of seronegative, refractory gMG highlighting the importance of expanding biological drugs eligibility to this group of patients.

Acta MyologicaActa MyolLong-term safety and efficacy of Eculizumab in generalized Myasthenia gravis: the 7 years’ experience of one patient at Sant’Andrea Hospital of Rome
LaulettaA TufanoL RossiniE MorinoS GaribaldiM FiondaL 1Neuromuscular and Rare Disease Centre, Department of Neuroscience, Mental Health and Sensory Organs (NESMOS), SAPIENZA University of Rome, Sant’Andrea Hospital, Rome, Italy162023421 Suppl 14141–113©2023 Gaetano Conte Academy - Mediterranean Society of Myology, Naples, ItalyThis is an open access article distributed in accordance with the CC-BY-NC-ND (Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International) license. ## Abstract
Generalized myasthenia gravis (MG) is an autoimmune disorder that leads to disabling weakness via damage to the neuromuscular junction. In most patients, the disease is mediated by autoantibodies to the acetylcholine receptor, which activate the complement cascade. Eculizumab, a late complement inhibitor, demonstrated therapeutic efficacy in these patients, however, data regarding its long-term impact is limited.
Therefore, we decided to evaluate the safety, tolerability and efficacy of 7-year treatment of Eculizumab in one patient with refractory anti-AChR-positive generalized MG.
Herein, we describe the case of a 62-year-old man with anti-AChR-positive generalized MG and history of several disease exacerbations, although multiple therapies administration. Due to poor control of disease symptoms, he was finally treated with Eculizumab and subsequently followed at our institution over 7- year period, exhibiting a satisfactory control of exacerbations compared to the pre-treatment period, until now. Moreover, in the last few months, due to personal circumstances, the patient increased the interval between infusions from 2-weeks to 3-weeks, maintaining clinical stability. A single serious adverse event (atrial fibrillation) was reported during the treatment period, not clearly related to the drug administration.
Eculizumab has been recently approved in the United States, Europe and Japan for the treatment of anti-AChR-positive generalized MG in adults. In our 7 years’ experience with one patient, Eculizumab has been shown to lead to good disease control and safety profile.
Even if further data are warranted, this case provides evidence for the long-term safety and sustained efficacy of Eculizumab for refractory gMG.
